                <h4>Guidelines - Part 3 - Occupational First Aid</h4>
<ul class = "navHorizPipe navRegTools"><li><a href="/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#87772020C04D4BD3818D605A1918E211"/>Related Regulations</a></li><li><a class='add_remove add_to_handbook' name='998F33D891434F2D915491565A68E1F9' id='998F33D891434F2D915491565A68E1F9' href='#'><span class='handbook_title'>+ Add to My Handbook</a></span></li></ul><a name="SectionNumber:G3.15-1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.15-1 Nurses acting as first aid attendants in health care facilities (formerly G3.15(b)-1)</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Editorial Revision consequential to November&nbsp;1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment; Editorial Revision July&nbsp;10, 2025</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.15 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The employer must ensure that a person who is designated as a first aid attendant</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) is at least 16 years old,</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) [Repealed]</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) has a first aid certificate in good standing at the required level issued by the Board or a person recognized by the Board, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) meets any other requirements determined by the Board for designation as a first aid attendant.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to outline the conditions under which a WorkSafeBC-approved first aid training provider may issue an intermediate first aid certificate to a registered nurse or registered psychiatric nurse, without requiring the nurse to first attend an occupational first aid training course. (Refer to OHS Guideline G3.15-2 for alternative guidance for nurses working for B.C. health authorities.)</p>
<p><strong>Requirement</strong><br />
WorkSafeBC-approved first aid training providers may issue an intermediate first aid certificate to a registered nurse or registered psychiatric nurse on receipt of a written request from an employer offering health care services confirming that:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The individual is currently employed, primarily as a practicing nurse, and holds a valid license issued by the BC College of Nurses and Midwives (BCCNM)</li>
    <li>The nurse has reviewed and completed the WorkSafeBC <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">jurisprudence package</a> or received other equivalent training</li>
    <li>The nurse has successfully completed a Basic Life Support course within the past 3 years</li>
</ul>
<p>The intermediate first aid certificates issued to nurses are not transferable to other industries.</p>
<p>Nurses are obligated by the professional standard of their licence to function only within their level of competence. Employers that wish to utilize registered nurses as first aid attendants under this provision must first ensure the nurse:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Is willing to act in the capacity of a first aid attendant </li>
    <li>Confirms they have been trained in the knowledge and skills associated with intermediate first aid attendants<sup>*</sup></li>
    <li>Has operational capacity to fulfil the duties required by section 3.21 of the <em>Regulation</em></li>
</ul>
<p><sup>*</sup> Skills and competencies for intermediate first aid attendants can be found in Appendix&nbsp;G of the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">jurisprudence package.</a></p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.15-1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.15-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.15-2 Registered nurses working for health authorities</h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment; Editorial Revision July&nbsp;10, 2025</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.15 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The employer must ensure that a person who is designated as a first aid attendant</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) is at least 16 years old,</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) [Repealed]</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) has a first aid certificate in good standing at the required level issued by the Board or a person recognized by the Board, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) meets any other requirements determined by the Board for designation as a first aid attendant.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to enable nurses to act as intermediate first aid attendants in specific health care facilities, without a first aid certificate. This guideline outlines the requirements under which WorkSafeBC will accept a nurse's licence as an alternative acceptable first aid credential.</p>
<p><strong>Acceptance of registered nurse and registered psychiatric nurse licences as intermediate first aid certificates in limited health care settings</strong><br />
Registered nurses (RNs) and registered psychiatric nurses (RPNs) are trained in wound care and basic lifesaving interventions as part of the licensing competencies required by the British Columbia College of Nurses and Midwives (BCCNM).</p>
<p>WorkSafeBC has entered into arrangements with the B.C. health authorities and Providence Health Care, as permitted by section 55 of the<em> Workers Compensation Act,</em> to recognize BCCNM licences as first aid certificates in limited health care locations directly operated by these specific employers. This provision applies only to the following health care workplaces:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Acute care workplaces assigned to WorkSafeBC classification unit 766001</li>
    <li>Long-term care workplaces assigned to WorkSafeBC classification unit 7660011</li>
    <li>Short-term care workplaces assigned to WorkSafeBC classification unit 766019</li>
    <li>Remote clinics operated by the First Nations Health Authority</li>
</ul>
<p>This provision does not apply to health care locations operated by other employers or other classification units.</p>
<p><strong>Requirement</strong><br />
Health authorities may utilize RNs or RPNs as occupational first aid attendants &mdash; intermediate level &mdash; in health care locations directly operated by a health authority or Providence Health Care. To use nurses as first aid attendants, the health authority or its delegate must document that the respective nurse  meets and maintains all of the following requirements:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Holds a valid RN or RPN licence from the British Columbia College of Nurses and Midwives</li>
    <li>Has reviewed and completed the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">jurisprudence package</a> or completed other training that covers the topics outlined in the jurisprudence package prior to commencing work as a first aid attendant</li>
    <li>Has completed a Basic Life Support course within the past 3 years</li>
</ul>
<p>Nurses are obligated by the professional standard of their licence to function only within their level of competence. Employers that wish to utilize registered nurses as first aid attendants under this provision must also ensure the nurse:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Agrees to act in the capacity of a first aid attendant </li>
    <li>Confirms they have been trained in the knowledge and skills associated with intermediate first aid attendants<sup>*</sup></li>
    <li>Has operational capacity to fulfil the duties required by section&nbsp;3.21 of the <em>Regulation</em></li>
</ul>
<p><sup>* </sup>Skills and competencies for intermediate first aid attendants can be found in Appendix G of the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">jurisprudence package.</a> Nurses are obligated by the professional standard of their licence to function only within their level of competence.</p>
<p>Health authority employers operating under this guidance are expected to implement an effective system to ensure these requirements are maintained for as long as the nurse is expected to provide first aid services to workers. </p>
<p>At the request of a WorkSafeBC officer, an employer must produce documentation verifying that the respective RN has met the requirements for licence recognition. This may be in the form of a letter from a health authority or its delegate.</p>
<p>Health care services providers that  use nurses as first aid attendants must comply with all other first aid requirements under the <em>Regulation</em>, including designating first aid attendants and developing written first aid procedures.</p>
<p><strong>Suspending or revoking acceptance of alternative licences</strong><br />
WorkSafeBC may revoke the acceptance of an individual BCCNM licence holder as a first aid attendant without impacting the validity of their medical licence. Medical licences issued by BCCNM may only be revoked or suspended by BCCNM. Practice concerns that come to the attention of WorkSafeBC will be referred to the licensing board for investigation and appropriate remedy.</p>
<p>Refer to <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.21_1">OHS Guideline G3.21(1)</a> for additional details.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.15-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.15_b-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.15(b)-2 Municipal fire departments</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Retired November 1, 2024</p>
<p>This guideline is now incorporated in G3.15-3.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.15_b-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.15-3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.15-3 EMA licence holders working as first aid attendants (formerly G3.15(b)-2 and G3.15(b)-3)</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Revised consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment; Editorial Revision July&nbsp;10, 2025</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.15 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states, in part:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The employer must ensure that a person who is designated as a first aid attendant</p>
<p class="subL2">&hellip;</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) has a first aid certificate in good standing at the required level issued by the Board or a person recognized by the Board, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) meets any other requirements determined by the Board for designation as a first aid attendant.</p>
<p class="subL2">&hellip;</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to enable Emergency Medical Assistant (EMA) licence holders to act as first aid attendants, without a first aid certificate issued by WorkSafeBC or a person recognized by WorkSafeBC. This guideline outlines the requirements under which WorkSafeBC will accept a licence issued by the Emergency Medical Assistants Licensing Board (EMALB) as an alternative acceptable first aid credential.</p>
<p><strong>Acceptance of Emergency Medical Assistant (EMA) licences as first aid certificates</strong><br />
Wound care and basic lifesaving interventions are included in the licensing competencies required by the British Columbia Emergency Medical Assistance Licensing Board (EMALB).</p>
<p>The scope of practice for EMAs includes the first aid competencies outlined in <em>CSA Z1210-17 First aid training for the workplace &mdash; curriculum and quality management for training agencies</em>.</p>
<p>WorkSafeBC has entered into arrangements with the Emergency Medical Assistant Licensing Board (EMALB) as permitted by section 55 of the Act. Recognition of EMALB licences as an alternative first aid credential is permitted in all occupational settings, under the conditions outlined below.</p>
<p><strong>Requirement</strong><br />
The following EMA licences are recognized alternative qualifications for occupational first aid attendants:</p>
<ul>
    <li>First Responder (EMA FR) &mdash; intermediate first aid certificate plus transportation endorsement</li>
    <li>Emergency Medical Responder (EMR) &mdash; advanced first aid certificate</li>
    <li>Primary Care Paramedic (PCP) &mdash; advanced first aid certificate</li>
    <li>Advanced Care Paramedic (ACP) &mdash; advanced first aid certificate </li>
    <li>Critical Care Paramedic (CCP) &mdash; advanced first aid certificate</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Employer obligations when employing first aid attendants with an EMA licence</strong><br />
Employers who deploy an EMA licence holder as a first aid attendant must ensure the licence holder has a working understanding of their responsibilities as an occupational first aid attendant. Employers may elect to do one of the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Have first aid attendants with alternative qualification complete the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">Jurisprudence package</a> prior to commencing work</li>
    <li>Provide on-the-job training that covers the topics outlined in the Jurisprudence package</li>
    <li>Have evidence that the EMA licence holder previously completed the jurisprudence package</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Operational scope of EMALB licence holders</strong><br />
EMALB licences used in industry are subject to operational limitations (refer to the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www2.gov.bc.ca/assets/gov/health/about-bc-s-health-care-system/heath-care-partners/colleges-board-and-commissions/emergency-medical-assistants-licensing-board/emalb_position_statement_-_operational_vs_legal_scope_of_practice.pdf" target="_blank">EMALB Position Statement on Operational vs. Legal Scope of Practice</a>). Workplaces that employ EMAs as first aid attendants are expected to restrict their scope of practice to align with the competencies listed in CSA Z1210-17 for advanced first aid attendants, unless medical oversight by a physician registered with the College of Physicians and Surgeons of BC is in place, along with supplemental written procedures. Operational scope should clarify the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>First aid attendants may not administer prescription drugs or medications. First aid attendants may assist workers to self-administer medication for which they have a prescription if the patient provides a letter from their physician. First aid attendants may provide injured workers with non-prescription (over-the-counter) medications purchased by the employer for use at the worksite, if appropriate to do so.<sup>*</sup></li>
    <li>First aid attendants must initiate immediate transfer to hospital for any injured worker who meets rapid transport criteria.<sup>*</sup></li>
</ul>
<p><sup>*</sup> Refer to <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/occupational_first_aid_reference_and_training_manual" target="_blank">Advanced First Aid: A Reference and Training Manual</a> for additional details on these requirements.</p>
<p><strong>Suspending or revoking acceptance of alternative licences</strong><br />
WorkSafeBC may revoke the acceptance of an individual EMA licence holder as a first aid attendant without impacting the validity of the medical licence. Medical licences issued by EMALB may only be revoked or suspended by EMALB, and practice concerns that come to the attention of WorkSafeBC will be referred to the licensing board for investigation and appropriate remedy.</p>
<p>Refer to <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.21_1">OHS Guideline G3.21(1)</a> for additional details on suspending and revoking alternative licences.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.15-3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.15_c" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.15(c) Proof of certification</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Revised consequential to November&nbsp;1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment; Editorial Revision July&nbsp;10, 2025</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.15 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states, in part:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The employer must ensure that a person who is designated as a first aid attendant</p>
<p class="subL2">&hellip;</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) has a first aid certificate in good standing at the required level issued by the Board or a person recognized by the Board, and</p>
<p class="subL2">&hellip;</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline is to outline the requirements for proof of certification at the workplace.</p>
<p><strong>Requirement</strong><br />
Before assigning a first aid attendant to duty, employers must take reasonable measures to confirm that a worker has a valid first aid certificate (or other credential accepted under the provisions of section 3.15) that is in good standing and accepted by WorkSafeBC. First aid certificates and other credentials accepted by WorkSafeBC are listed on worksafebc.com: <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/health-safety/create-manage/first-aid-requirements/certificates-accepted-in-bc" target="_blank">Certificates accepted in B.C.</a></p>
<p>The status of a licence issued by the Emergency Medical Assistants Licensing Board (EMALB) can be confirmed by either obtaining a licence status report from EMALB or reviewing the report posted on <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www2.gov.bc.ca/gov/content/health/about-bc-s-health-care-system/partners/colleges-boards-and-commissions/emergency-medical-assistants-licensing-board/licensing" target="_blank">Licensing - Province of British Columbia.</a></p>
<p>Employers are expected to keep a copy of the attendant's first aid credential, and provide it to a WorkSafeBC officer upon request. It is recommended that employers track the expiration date of first aid credentials to ensure first aid attendants recertify when required. Where practical to do so, first aid credentials should be posted at the workplace for review by workers who may receive first aid.</p>
<p>First aid attendants must produce their original credential or a verifiable digital credential to their employer prior to their first shift as a first aid attendant. Attendants must also produce their credential to a WorkSafeBC officer upon request within a reasonable period. </p>
<p>Acceptable verifiable digital certificates permit the validity of the certificate to be confirmed through an online database maintained by the training agency. Employers should not accept digital photographs of certificates, where the authenticity of the certificate cannot be validated. Digital photographs may be accepted by WorkSafeBC officers as sufficient evidence of training, if the employer can confirm the certificate is valid. </p>
<p>First aid attendants are expected to notify their employer of any changes to the status of their licence or certification that could impact their ability to provide first aid services.
</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.15_c" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16 First aid assessment</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Revised February 1, 2008; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018; Editorial revision October&nbsp;30, 2018; Editorial Revision January&nbsp;17, 2022; Revised consequential to November&nbsp;1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.16 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) An employer must provide for each workplace</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) at least the equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services required by Schedule 3-A, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) any additional equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services that are necessary to ensure that workers who suffer an injury at work can be</p>
<p class="subL3">(i)  promptly provided first aid, and</p>
<p class="subL3">(ii)  promptly transported to medical treatment.</p>
<p class="subL1">(2) For the purpose of complying with subsection (1)(b), the employer must prepare a written assessment that sets out at least the following information:</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) the number of workers present;</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the locations of workers;</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) the nature and extent of the risk and hazards in the workplace;</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) the types of injuries likely to occur;</p>
<p class="subL2">(e) any barriers to first aid being provided to an injured worker;</p>
<p class="subL2">(f) the time that may be required to obtain transportation and to transport an injured worker to medical treatment and the methods of transportation available.</p>
<p class="subL1">(3) The employer must review and update the assessment under subsection&nbsp;(2)</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) within 12 months after the previous assessment or review, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) whenever a significant change affecting the assessment occurs in the employer's operations.</p>
<p class="subL1">(3.1) An assessment under subsection (2) must be prepared, reviewed and updated in consultation with the joint committee or the worker health and safety representative, as applicable.</p>
<p class="subL1">(4) An employer must ensure that the equipment, supplies and facilities required by subsection&nbsp;(1) are</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) suitable for their intended use,</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) clean, dry and ready for use, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) readily accessible</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline explains the process for determining adequate first aid coverage. This guidance is designed to help employers establish which table in <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#Schedule3A">Schedule 3-A &mdash; Minimum Levels of First Aid</a> sets out the minimum first aid services for their workplace, and provides clarity on additional factors that must be considered when determining first aid services. </p>
<p>For the purpose of this guideline, the term "first aid services" should be taken to include first aid equipment, supplies, facilities, and first aid attendants.</p>
<p><strong>Employers' two-part duty</strong><br />
Section 3.16(1) sets out a two-part duty to determine the level of first aid supplies required at any given workplace. Section 3.16(1)(a) requires an employer to determine the minimum levels of first aid based on the employer's hazard rating, location and nature of workplace, and number of workers. Section 3.16(1)(b) requires employers to fully evaluate any circumstances unique to the workplace to determine any additional first aid equipment, supplies, facilities, attendants, and services necessary to ensure compliance with this regulation. Consideration of both parts must be done in consultation with the joint health and safety committee or worker health and safety representative, and documented in a written first aid assessment required by section 3.16(2).</p>
<p><strong>Determining minimum first aid requirements &mdash; Section 3.16(1)(a)</strong><br />
Minimum first aid services are determined by assessing three factors:</p>
<ol>
    <li>The number of workers present at the workplace</li>
    <li>Hazard rating </li>
    <li>The class of workplace (which includes consideration of both the remoteness and the accessibility of the workplace)</li>
</ol>
<a name="SectionNumber:Step1"></a>
<p><strong>Step 1: Number of workers present at the workplace</strong></p>
<p>When determining minimum first aid requirements, employers should calculate the maximum number of workers present at the workplace during a typical shift. All workers &mdash; including managers, supervisors, and administrative staff who are regularly present at the workplace &mdash; must be included in the worker count under this provision, regardless of whether they are normally exposed to the substantive hazards of the workplace. </p>
<p>Where the number of workers consistently varies between shifts, separate assessments may be conducted for each shift.</p>
<p>Section 13 of the <em>Workers Compensation Act</em> defines a "workplace" as "any place where a worker is or is likely to be engaged in any work and includes any vessel, vehicle or mobile equipment used by a worker in work."</p>
<p>Consider the following factors when determining if an area or building is part of a single workplace, or may be considered a separate workplace for the purpose of establishing first aid requirements:</p>
<p>Factors supporting a single workplace:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Fixed work locations under the control of a single employer</li>
    <li>Multiple-employer workplaces where a prime contractor has been established</li>
</ul>
<p>Factors supporting separate workplaces:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Locations leased by one employer that are part of a larger property leased to others</li>
    <li>Locations controlled by one employer that are separated by locations controlled by other employers</li>
    <li>Locations of one employer that are more than 10 minutes apart from each other, or where physical barriers might prevent a first aid attendant from reaching an injured worker in less than 10 minutes (note that this factor should not be used to decrease minimum first aid requirements in high-risk remote industries, such as forestry, where workers work in small groups over large areas)</li>
    <li>A public roadway separates locations of one employer from each other in an urban area</li>
    <li>Locations under separate administrative structures, controlled by one employer</li>
</ul>
<p>In any situation, the factors may point to different conclusions. It is then necessary to weigh those factors indicating one workplace against those indicating separate workplaces, considering the workplace structure and relative risk to workers. After considering all the factors, the option that provides an appropriate level of first aid service should be chosen.</p>
<p><strong>Determining number of workers for workplaces that dispatch workers or that have transient workers</strong></p>
<p>All workers who regularly attend a workplace must be included in the worker count for that workplace, even if they are on site only briefly.</p>
<p>When determining the minimum first aid requirements for a workplace, employers should count dispatched or transient workers as follows:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Count as one worker each dispatched worker who stays within 10 minutes' surface travel time from the workplace for more than 50% of the shift.</li>
    <li>Count one-quarter of the number of workers who travel more than 10 minutes' surface travel time from the workplace more than 50% of the shift.</li>
    <li>Pro-rate the number of transient workers based on an estimation of the time they attend the workplace. For example, if a warehouse has eight loading bays that are serviced by a fleet of delivery trucks throughout the day, the employer may determine that it is reasonable to include an additional eight workers for the purpose of determining first aid requirements at the warehouse, if that is the maximum number of delivery drivers that are likely to be at the workplace at any given time.</li>
</ul>
<p>Separate first aid assessment(s) are required for dispatched workers when they are not present at the central location.</p>
<p>It is recommended that personal first aid kits are present in all workplace vehicles that travel further than 10 minutes from the central workplace, unless a larger kit is required by Schedule 3-A, a first aid assessment, or the <em>Motor Vehicle Act</em>.</p>
<p>Other requirements for worker transportation under <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-17-transportation-of-workers">Part 17</a> of the <em>Regulation</em> must also be met.</p>
<a name="SectionNumber:Step2"></a>
<p><strong>Step 2: Determine your hazard rating</strong></p>
<p>"Hazard rating" is defined in section 1(1) of Schedule 3-A as "how hazardous the workplace is, expressed as low, moderate, or high, relative to all other workplaces." For the convenience of stakeholders, WorkSafeBC assigns a relative hazard rating to each classification unit (CU) based on claims statistics and knowledge of common risks and hazards typically associated with workplaces assigned to the CU.</p>
<p>Hazard ratings assigned by WorkSafeBC can be found on the classification unit description sent to employers with their assessments rate letter every year. Hazard ratings can also be accessed on worksafebc.com: <a href="/en/insurance/know-coverage-costs/find-classification-industry-rate" target="_blank">Find your classification unit, industry, or rate,</a> or <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/resources/health-safety/information-sheets/workplace-hazard-ratings" target="_blank">Workplace hazard ratings.</a></p>
<p>The hazard rating assigned by WorkSafeBC should be considered the minimum acceptable hazard rating for workplaces of employers assigned to that CU, with the following exceptions:</p>
<p><strong>Separate administrative locations</strong> &ndash; Administrative locations that meet the criteria of being separate workplaces (refer to step 1), where the substantive hazards of the industry are not present may consider themselves a low hazard workplace for the purpose of determining minimum first aid requirements.</p>
<p><strong>Multiple-employer workplaces where work is conducted in stages</strong> &ndash; Where the prime contractor of a multiple-employer workplace is providing first aid services on a project where work is conducted in stages. For example:</p>
<ul>
    <li>A prime contractor in Integrated forestry management (CU 703008) with a hazard rating of high is providing first aid services when the only work being conducted is tree planting with hazard rating of medium &mdash; a hazard rating of medium may be used.</li>
    <li>A prime contractor in Industrial general construction (CU 721028) with a hazard rating of high is providing first aid services when the only work being conducted is window installation with hazard rating of medium &mdash; a hazard rating of medium may be used.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Hazard rating for unassigned classification units</strong> &mdash; Where a hazard rating is not available for a particular classification unit (e.g., new classification units, deposit accounts), employers should use the hazard rating associated with a comparable and aligned CU to determine minimum first aid services.</p>
<p><strong>Using hazard ratings for alternative classification units</strong> &mdash; Employers should consider using the hazard rating associated with an alternative classification unit under the following circumstances:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Where the work being performed at a separate workplace is not generally consistent with work described in the employer's classification unit description and poses a greater risk to workers (e.g., a home renovation store performing installation services on a construction site). Classification unit descriptions can be found on worksafebc.com: <a href="/en/insurance/know-coverage-costs/find-classification-industry-rate" target="_blank">Find your classification unit, industry, or rate.</a></li>
    <li>Where "supporting activities" conducted in support of the main business activity are conducted at a separate workplace and pose a greater risk to workers than the main business activity (e.g., a warehouse or distribution location of a large retail employer)</li>
    <li>Large employers with a variety of functional groups operating at separate workplaces (e.g., municipalities, school districts, utility providers)</li>
</ul>
<p>Using a hazard rating associated with an alternative CU must not be used to reduce an employer's hazard rating during certain tasks, times, or areas of a workplace.</p>
<p>The decision to use the hazard rating for an alternative classification unit should be made in consultation with worker representatives and the employer's joint committee, where these resources exist. Justification for the selection of an alternative classification unit must be adequately documented on the written first aid assessment required by section 3.16(2).</p>
<p>Selecting an alternative classification unit for the purpose of determining minimum first aid levels has no bearing on the classification unit assigned to the employer for the purpose of WorkSafeBC assessments.</p>
<a name="SectionNumber:Step3"></a>
<p><strong>Step 3: Determine the workplace class (remote and/or less accessible)</strong></p>
<p>There are two primary factors that must be assessed when considering the location of workers: remoteness and accessibility to BC Emergency Health Services (BC Ambulance Service). The minimum first aid requirements established by Schedule 3-A combine these two factors to define workplace class:</p>
<ul>
    <li>A "class 1 workplace" means a workplace other than a class 2, 3, or 4 workplace</li>
    <li>A "class 2 workplace" means a workplace that is a remote workplace but not a less-accessible workplace</li>
    <li>A "class 3 workplace" means a workplace that is a less-accessible workplace but not a
    remote workplace
    </li>
    <li>A "class 4 workplace" means a workplace that is both a remote workplace and a less-accessible workplace</li>
</ul>
<p><em>Remote workplaces</em> are defined under Schedule 3-A to be those workplaces which, under normal travel conditions, an ambulance that is a motor vehicle cannot travel from its base in 30 minutes or less. "Normal travel conditions" should be determined by the average time it takes to drive to (or from) the nearest ambulance station by a motor vehicle following the rules of the road, during the normal working hours of the employer's workplace (refer to "Determining additional first aid service requirements" later in this guideline for additional considerations on normal travel time).</p>
<p>The location of the nearest BCEHS ambulance station, if not already known, can be found using online mapping tools by searching for BCEHS ambulance station.</p>
<p>Less-accessible workplaces are defined under Schedule 3-A as workplaces that include work areas that cannot be safely accessed by ambulance personnel. Less-accessible workplaces include both workplaces that cannot readily be reached by an ambulance travelling by land, as well as those where an ambulance attendant may not be able to access an injured worker safely. BCEHS will generally not access the following areas:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Backcountry areas that are only accessible by ATV, snowmobile, or similar</li>
    <li>Areas where the only means of access involves steep or slippery slopes or embankments (without walkways)</li>
    <li>Areas with rough or complex terrain </li>
    <li>Areas where there is a significant risk of avalanche, landslide, flood, or other natural hazards</li>
</ul>
<p>Where workers can reasonably be expected to be working in these areas, the workplace should be considered less-accessible for the purpose of determining minimum first aid requirements. </p>
<p>
<p>BCEHS will also not rescue injured workers from hazardous work areas such as: </p>
<ul>
    <li>Confined spaces or where there is a risk of entrapment</li>
    <li>Underground work</li>
    <li>Excavations</li>
    <li>Areas that are only accessible by ladders, scaffolds, or temporary work platforms</li>
    <li>Work at high angles, or where an unguarded fall hazard exists</li>
    <li>Areas where a drowning hazard exists (work on or over water)</li>
    <li>Areas requiring specialized PPE or areas where hazardous atmospheres may exist</li>
</ul>
<p>Workplaces where workers may be working in hazardous areas should also be considered less accessible, unless:</p>
<ol>
    <li>The workplace is not remote, nor otherwise less accessible, and</li>
    <li>The employer has developed provisions to safely rescue workers. For the purpose of this section, "safely rescue" will be taken to mean:
    <ul>
        <li>Moving the injured worker from the hazardous area to an area accessible to BCEHS in a way that will not cause additional injury or further exacerbate pre-existing injuries or illnesses. This will generally include packaging and transporting an injured worker in accordance with the practices taught in the transportation endorsement course, unless impractical to do so (for example, for workers working in a bucket truck that can be lowered to the ground, or workers working in confined spaces who remain on a harness that allows them to be pulled from the hazardous area).</li>
        <li>Where practicable to do so, and without putting the first aid attendant at unreasonable risk, nor delaying transport to an area accessible to BCEHS, provide immediate and on-going first aid to the injured worker during the rescue. The level of first aid should match the hazards of the less-accessible area.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ol>
<p>Refer to <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-04#SectionNumber:G4.13_1">G4.13(1)</a> for additional information on emergency response.</p>
<p>Workplaces located on roads that cannot be made accessible to BCEHS ambulance must be considered both less-accessible and remote.</p>
<p>Ferries are defined as class 3 workplaces for the purpose of determining minimum first aid requirements for workers who work in non-public areas of the vessel. Travel by ferry where workers remain only in public areas may be considered not-less accessible. For workplaces that require ferry transport to drive to the nearest BCEHS station, ferry travel time (including potential wait times) should be considered equivalent to surface travel time for the purpose of determining whether the workplace is remote or not.</p>
<p>Other marine vessels should also be considered less accessible workplaces when they are away from dock. Marine vessels, aside from ferries should be considered remote if it will take longer than 30 minutes for an ambulance to reach the nearest docking location accessible to BCEHS. For vessels that may work in remote locations, Coast Guard services may be considered as emergency transport, where this service is available.</p>
<a name="SectionNumber:Step4"></a>
<p><strong>Step 4: Determine minimum first aid requirements in Schedule 3-A</strong></p>
<p>Minimum first aid requirements for a workplace are determined by referring to Tables 3-1 to 3-4 in Schedule 3-A, using the information established in Steps 1, 2, and 3 above:</p>
<p class="subL1">a)	Determine which Schedule 3-A reference table to use based on the class of workplace established in Step 3:</p>
<table class="tableData" border="1" cellspacing="2" cellpadding="2">
    <tbody>
        <tr bgcolor="#cccccc">
            <th width="25%">&nbsp;</th>
            <th colspan="2">Description from Step 3</th>
            <th width="25%">&nbsp;</th>
        </tr>
        <tr bgcolor="#cccccc">
            <th width="25%">Class</th>
            <th width="25%">Greater than 30 minutes from ambulance base?</th>
            <th width="25%">Less-accessible workplace?</th>
            <th width="25%">Schedule 3-A reference table</th>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Class 1</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">No</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">No</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">3-1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Class 2</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Yes</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">No</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">3-2</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Class 3</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">No</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Yes</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">3-3</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Class 4</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Yes</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">Yes</td>
            <td style="width: 25%;">3-4</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<br />
<p class="subL1">b)	Identify the correct row of the table based on the number of workers at the workplace established in Step 1.</p>
<p class="subL1">c)	Identify the correct column of the table based on the hazard rating of the workplace established in Step 2.</p>
<p class="subL1">d)	Determine the minimum requirements for first aid kits, first aid attendants, and emergency transportation requirements (where required) identified in the intersecting cell of the table.</p>
<p>Additional information on the requirements of first aid kits, first aid facilities, and emergency transport can be found in OHS Guideline <a href="#SectionNumber:G3.16_1">G3.16(1) Minimum requirements for first aid equipment.</a> Where "(transport)" is indicated next to the requirement for a first aid attendant, the attendant must also obtain transportation endorsement by attending an additional 8-hour training course. Where a requirement for emergency transportation is identified, the number of workers capable of being transported at the same time will also be specified.</p>
<p><strong>Determining additional first aid service requirements &mdash; Section 3.16(1)(b)</strong><br />
In addition to determining the minimum requirements for first aid, employers must also consider the unique circumstances of the workplace to verify that the minimum requirements are sufficient to meet the regulatory requirement of ensuring injured workers can be promptly provided first aid and transport to medical treatment. </p>
<p>The following additional factors must be considered as part of the employer's first aid assessment:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Nature and extent of risk and hazards in the workplace<br />
    Employers are expected to enhance the minimum requirements for first aid services set out by Schedule 3-A based on specific hazards and risks present at the employer's workplace. More information about identifying hazards in the workplace can be found on worksafebc.com: Identifying hazards. Employers must consider enhancing first aid services when hazards are identified that are unique to the workplace, workers, or not typical of the employer's classification unit (refer to table below for examples).</li>
    <li>Types of injuries likely to occur<br />
    Employers are expected to consider the types of injuries that may reasonably be expected at the workplace to ensure that minimum first aid services are adequate to promptly treat and transport workers (refer to table below for examples). Information on the types of injuries that have previously occurred at the employer's workplace or similar workplaces can be found in:
    <ul>
        <li>Past first aid records, incident reports, and claims history</li>
        <li>Industry <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/contact-us/related-organizations/health-safety-associations" target="_blank">health and safety associations</a></li>
        <li>WorkSafeBC statistics (refer to <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/about-us/shared-data/industry-health-safety-data" target="_blank">Industry health and safety data)</a></li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<p>Examples of hazards and risks that may warrant the consideration of additional equipment, supplies, facilities, and/or attendants:</p>
<table class="tableData" border="1" cellspacing="2" cellpadding="2">
    <tbody>
        <tr bgcolor="#cccccc">
            <th>Hazard or risk</th>
            <th>Additional first aid services</th>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Possible exposure to chemicals or toxins whose safety data sheet recommends the use of specific first aid measures to counteract toxic effects</td>
            <td>Maintain neutralizing compounds, or antidotes (e.g., calcium gluconate for the treatment of hydrofluoric acid burns)</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Probability of intense physical exertion, or risk of exposure to electrical hazards</td>
            <td>Consider providing and maintaining an Automatic External Defibrillator (AED). All first aid attendants are trained to use AEDs generally, but will need additional training on the specific model(s) available in the workplace.</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Likely contact with hot surfaces or liquids</td>
            <td>Maintain cooling packs, specialty burn dressings, and/or other suitable treatment options (e.g., hydrogel, burn sheets)</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Occupational risk factors that could lead to psychological injury</td>
            <td>Consider training first aid attendants (or other workers) on recognizing symptoms of psychological injuries and the appropriate response to mental health emergencies </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Workers work at heights or around hazards that are likely to result in spinal injuries in areas where BCEHS response may be delayed</td>
            <td>Provide equipment for spinal immobilization and transportation (ETV equipment), and train attendants in the appropriate procedures</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Workers may be exposed to extreme temperatures</td>
            <td>Maintain equipment suitable to treat hypothermia (cold stress) or hyperthermia (heat stress), as applicable</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<br />
<p class="indent">If an employer chooses to provide naloxone at the workplace to treat over-exposure to opioids, the employer must ensure first aid attendants have received training in the appropriate and safe use of this substance. Some first aid courses may provide instruction in the administration of naloxone.</p>
<ul>
    <li>Barriers to first aid <br />
    Employers must consider additional barriers that could potentially limit or delay a worker's access to first aid. Examples of potential barriers may include:</li>
</ul>
<table class="tableData" border="1" cellspacing="2" cellpadding="2">
    <tbody>
        <tr bgcolor="#cccccc">
            <th>Barriers to first aid</th>
            <th>Possible means to address</th>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Physical obstacles or accessibility challenges first aid attendants may encounter at the workplace	</td>
            <td>
            <ul>
                <li>Eliminate obstacles</li>
                <li>Provide alternative means to access areas</li>
                <li>Embed first aid attendants into crews</li>
            </ul>
            </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Areas where workers may be unable to communicate their need for first aid or assistance (e.g., where workers may work alone or in isolation, or in areas without access to phones or other means of communication)	</td>
            <td>
            <ul>
                <li>Provide alternative means of communication</li>
                <li>Assign workers to work with others</li>
                <li>Provide regular check-ins (refer to sections 4.20.1 &ndash; 4.22 of the <em>Regulation</em> for more information)</li>
            </ul>
            </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Workplace is large and workers work over large areas </td>
            <td>Consider the number and distribution of first aid attendants and equipment to ensure timely response (10-minute walking time) to all areas of the workplace. Provide additional first aid supplies as necessary.</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Where the employer expects first aid attendants to treat members of the public or others who may be present at the workplace, who were not counted in the number of workers used to determine minimum first aid requirements</td>
            <td>Ensure the number of first aid attendants and procedures are sufficient to provide treatment to workers without undue delay</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Language or cultural barriers between first aid attendants and injured workers	</td>
            <td>Consider providing additional first aid attendants or additional training to existing attendants and/or workers to reduce perceived or actual barriers</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Other hazardous areas that require specialized training or controls to access</td>
            <td>Provide additional training or equipment to first aid attendants to enhance access to hazardous areas</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<br />
<p class="indent">Where barriers are identified, it is expected that employers will take all reasonable efforts to eliminate, reduce, or otherwise address those barriers through the provision of appropriate first aid equipment, procedures, and trained personnel.</p>
<ul>
    <li>Specialized equipment required for worker transportation and evacuation<br />
    For less-accessible workplaces, employers are expected to ensure workers can be rescued from hazardous areas and safely moved to an area accessible to BCEHS personnel. Where emergency transportation or transport endorsement of first aid attendants is required by Schedule 3-A, employers should maintain equipment necessary to safely move an injured worker. This will normally include the ETV equipment listed in OHS Guideline G3.16(1) Emergency transportation unless alternative means of safe transport is provided.</li>
    <li>The time that may be required to obtain transportation and to transport an injured worker to medical treatment and the methods of transportation available<br />
    Employers must also consider known barriers that may affect BCEHS response time, or time to transport to a hospital such as:
    <ul>
        <li>Road or weather conditions that may make a workplace inaccessible to BCEHS or may prevent air transportation from flying.</li>
        <li>Barriers between workplace and hospital or ambulance station (such as train crossings, lift bridges, temporary road closures, construction zones), if there is no other method of accessing the workplace and these could result in significant delays.</li>
        <li>Hours of operation, doctor availability, hospital diversion protocols, or routine closures of BCEHS stations that would significantly impact transport time to hospital.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<p class="indent">These factors may be temporary or permanent, but when they are known or reasonably foreseeable they should be factored into the employer's first aid assessment. "Reasonably foreseeable" includes those that are known to the employer or workers through previous experience or observation, or that are widely publicized in the news or social media outlets.</p>
<p class="indent">Where barriers to transport are identified, the employer should take all reasonable measures to eliminate or otherwise minimize the impact of these barriers on the time it may take to transport a worker to medical treatment. Measures may include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Providing supplemental emergency transportation to safely transport an injured worker to hospital or to meet BCEHS enroute</li>
    <li>Establishing alternative methods of accessing the workplace</li>
    <li>Providing a higher level of first aid attendant and equipment to better manage injuries while waiting for BCEHS</li>
</ul>
<p class="indent">First aid procedures should address known or reasonably foreseeable barriers to transport (e.g., in the event of a worker injury, consider hospital emergency room availability in transport decisions).</p>
<p><strong>Documenting the first aid assessment</strong><br />
A written first aid assessment detailing the employer's consideration of the factors for both section 3.16(1)(a) and section 3.16(1)(b) must be available for every workplace. The employer's written assessment must document both the minimum first aid services required and any additional services provided to address the requirements under section 3.16(1)(b). When documenting additional factors under section 3.16(b), employers may group hazards and/or only document the factors that require additional supplies or equipment necessary to ensure prompt first aid or transport to medical treatment.</p>
<p>The assessment must not result in a level of first aid services lower than the mandatory minimums required by Schedule 3-A, except as provided in Step 2 in this guideline.
WorkSafeBC has developed a <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/resources/health-safety/forms/first-aid-assessment-worksheet" target="_blank">first aid assessment worksheet</a> that may be used to document the employer's first aid assessments. An online first aid assessment tool is also available.</p>
<p><strong>First aid assessments for temporary work locations or for workers who are dispatched to various workplaces</strong><br />
Employers must consider how workers will receive first aid and be transported to medical care in the event of a serious injury, even when workers are working away from the employer's main location. This may mean coordinating first aid services with employers of other workplaces, or ensuring first aid services are available to respond to injuries that occur at temporary work locations. </p>
<p>Where workers perform similar tasks at many different locations throughout the day (e.g., delivery drivers, repair technicians, community care workers), employers may consider conducting a "task-based" first aid assessment that applies to multiple locations. Task-based assessments should only be used for tasks where the number of workers, nature and extent of risks, and other elements of the first aid assessment are similar. Task-based assessments are expected to be reviewed by worker health and safety representatives or joint health and safety committees, as applicable, prior to use.</p>
<p>Employers may also develop template assessment documents that enable workers to update the assessment with information specific to the workplace, as applicable.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16_1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16(1) Minimum requirements for first aid equipment (formerly G3.16(1.1))</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Revised June 3, 2020; Editorial Revision May&nbsp;31, 2021; Revised consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.16 of the <cite>Occupational Health and Safety Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) The type and quantity of equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services referred to in subsection&nbsp;(1) must be no less than is required by Schedule&nbsp;3-A.</p>
<p class="subL1">An employer must provide for each workplace </p>
<p class="subL1">(a) at least the equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services required by Schedule 3-A, and</p>
<p class="subL1">(b) any additional equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services that are necessary to ensure that workers who suffer an injury at work can be</p>
<p class="subL1">(i) promptly provided first aid, and</p>
<p class="subL1">(ii) promptly transported to medical treatment.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to outline the recommended type and quantity of equipment, supplies, and facilities as required by <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#Schedule3A">Schedule 3-A</a> of the <em>Regulation</em>. An assessment under section&nbsp;3.16(2) of the <em>Regulation</em> may indicate additional equipment. This may also involve ensuring additional training for the designated first aid attendant.</p>
<a name="FirstAidKits"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:FirstAidKits" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<p><strong>First aid kits</strong></p>
<p>The kit contents are aligned with the Canadian Standards Association standard CSA Z1220-17 First aid kits for the workplace. This information is also available in a resource on worksafebc.com: <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/resources/health-safety/information-sheets/first-aid-equipment-supplies-facilities" target="_blank">First aid equipment, supplies, and facilities.</a></p>
<p>Note that all workplace first aid kit contents should be made of latex-free materials. They must also be kept clean, dry, ready for use, and readily accessible.</p>
<p>Sizes given here are approximate as exact sizing varies by supplier.</p>

<p>* Diabetes Canada considers a dose to be 15 g of carbohydrate. A dose can consist of:</p>
<ul>
    <li>15 g of glucose in the form of glucose tablets</li>
    <li>15 mL (3 tsp.) or 3 packets of table sugar, dissolved in water</li>
    <li>5 cubes of sugar</li>
    <li>150 mL (2/3 cup) of juice or regular (non-diet) soft drink</li>
    <li>6 Life Saver candies or similar (1 piece = 2.5 g of carbohydrate)</li>
    <li>15 ml (1 tbsp.) of honey</li>
</ul>

<p>* Diabetes Canada considers a dose to be 15 g of carbohydrate. A dose can consist of:</p>
<ul>
    <li>15 g of glucose in the form of glucose tablets</li>
    <li>15 mL (3 tsp.) or 3 packets of table sugar, dissolved in water</li>
    <li>5 cubes of sugar</li>
    <li>150 mL of juice or regular (non-diet) soft drink</li>
    <li>6 Life Saver candies or similar (1 piece = 2.5 g of carbohydrate)</li>
    <li>15 mL (1 tbsp.) of honey</li>
</ul>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:FirstAidKits" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:FirstAidFacilities" style="padding: 0px;"></a><br />
<p><strong>First aid facilities</strong></p>
<p><strong>General recommendations for all first aid facilities</strong></p>
<p><em>Location and access</em><br />
A first aid facility should be located as near as practicable to the work area or areas it is to serve. It should be a room within a building or, if this is not practicable, a tent, vehicle, or other suitable structure.</p>
<p>The first aid facility should be designed and located for easy entrance to and exit from the facility for a worker requiring transportation by stretcher or alternative carrying device.</p>
<p>In remote areas, building a first aid facility may not be practicable. However, the facility should be at least of the same design and construction as workers' lodgings. If trailers are provided for workers' lodgings, a trailer should be provided for the first aid facility.</p>
<p>When a tent is used, it should adhere to the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Be of the same size and have the same equipment as a first aid room or dressing station, as appropriate</li>
    <li>Be fitted with a non-porous floor that can be cleaned with soap and water</li>
    <li>Have a source of heat that will not be a hazard when oxygen is in use and that will provide sufficient warmth for good patient care (maintaining body temperature)</li>
</ul>
<p>A first aid facility may be locked to prevent theft and vandalism or for other appropriate reasons. If so, there must be effective means of immediate access during all working hours.</p>
<p><em>Utilities</em><br />
The facility should be adequately ventilated, illuminated, and temperature controlled (heated and cooled) as required to maintain patient comfort. It should have a sink plumbed with hot and cold running water or, if this is not practicable, an alternative system for supplying fresh, potable water of a reasonable temperature. If showering may be a required treatment for chemical exposure, the facility should have a shower or have a shower facility as near as practicable. It may be impracticable to plumb a first aid facility in certain situations, such as where the facility is a trailer on a construction site or if the work is at a remote location.</p>
<p>One of the following alternative sources of water, with means to heat it, may be considered until a permanent source of water can be connected:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The facility has an internal tank able to hold a minimum of 45 litres (10 gallons) of fresh potable water that can be pumped into the facility's sink. The water in this tank must be changed daily, or changed weekly if treated for the prevention of contamination.</li>
    <li>The facility is connected to a hose or water line from a fresh potable water outlet that can be pumped into the facility's sink.</li>
    <li>The facility has an insulated container able to hold about 20 litres (5 gallons) of fresh potable water changed daily to prevent contamination.</li>
    <li>A fresh water supply company provides fresh water in a bottle or jug attached to a hot and cold dispenser.</li>
</ul>
<p><em>Other recommendations</em><br />
Since the facility must be kept clean and sanitary, a non-porous floor covering is recommended.</p>
<p>The facility should have a notice conspicuously displayed outside the door or in the area, indicating how to call and where to find the first aid attendant, and if necessary, how to unlock and access the facility.</p>
<p>The first aid facility is also subject to the general requirements relating to workplace premises in the <em>Regulation</em>, such as <a href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-06-substance-specific-requirements#SectionNumber:6.33">sections 6.33 to 6.41</a> (biological agents) and <a href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-04-general-conditions#SectionNumber:4.80.1">sections 4.80.1 to 4.83</a> (environmental tobacco smoke).</p>
<p>Smoking is not permitted in a first aid facility, and "No Smoking" signs should be conspicuously posted.</p>

            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Pillow with non-porous surface or covered with non-porous materials</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Portable urinal (if overnight care is required)</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Bedpan (if overnight care is required)</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Blank first aid records</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">&nbsp;</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Patient assessment charts</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">&nbsp;</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<p>Note: Where first aid kits are not readily accessible to the first aid room, additional PPE for first aid attendants and an additional CPR pocket mask with one-way valve should be provided in the first aid room.</p>
<p><strong>Using a first aid facility for purposes other than first aid</strong><br />
A first aid facility may be used for purposes other than first aid if the following exists:</p>
<ul>
    <li>It is immediately available for first aid treatment</li>
    <li>The facility is not at a workplace more than 2 hours' surface travel from a hospital</li>
    <li>The minimum floor area needed for first aid is maintained</li>
    <li>Such use will neither impede the treatment of an injured worker nor pose a hazard to workers</li>
</ul>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:first-aid-room" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:FirstAidFacilities" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:EmergencyVehicles" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:emergency-transportation" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<p><strong>Emergency transportation</strong></p>
<p><strong>General guidelines for emergency vehicles</strong><br />
Emergency vehicles used to transport workers must be designed, maintained, and operated in a safe manner in accordance with <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-17-transportation-of-workers">Part&nbsp;17</a> of the <em>Regulation</em>. Emergency vehicles designed to operate on public roads may also be subject to requirements under other statutes and regulations.</p>
<p>Smoking is not permitted in emergency vehicles and a plainly visible "No Smoking" sign should be posted in the vehicle.</p>
<p><em>Location and access</em><br />
Where a vehicle is needed to transport an injured worker, the vehicle should be immediately available for use and capable of being at the accident scene or location of pick-up within 10 minutes. It should be located where it will best serve the workers who are most likely to need an emergency vehicle.</p>
<p>The vehicle should be operated by someone other than the first aid attendant when an injured worker is being transported so that the attendant is able to provide treatment as needed.</p>
<p><strong>Emergency transport vehicle (ETV) requirements</strong></p>
<ul>
    <li>The ETV should be capable of traversing the area it is intended to serve.</li>
    <li>It should have a minimum headroom of 1 metre (3.3 feet).</li>
    <li>It must provide protection from natural elements and dust.</li>
    <li>It should provide warmth and cooling sufficient for good care for the injured worker, with the patient compartment able to maintain normal body temperature.</li>
    <li>The source of heat must not be a hazard to the occupants of the vehicle when oxygen is in use.</li>
    <li>It must have effective voice communication between the operator and the attendant in the treatment area of the vehicle.</li>
    <li>It should have a means of effective communication with the scene of an accident. For example:</li>
    <ul style="list-style-type: circle;">
        <li>The driver has a two-way radio that has a direct link with another two-way radio at the scene of the injured or ill worker.</li>
        <li>The driver has a two-way radio that has a link with the employer's central dispatch centre, which has voice communication via a radio or satellite phone with workers at the scene.</li>
        <li>In areas with good coverage, cell phones may be used.</li>
    </ul>
    <li>It should have effective communication with the hospital. For example:</li>
    <ul style="list-style-type: circle;">
        <li>The driver has a two-way radio that has a direct link with the hospital or BC Emergency Health Services (BCEHS).</li>
        <li>A radio or satellite telephone in the vehicle can contact the hospital or BCEHS directly.</li>
        <li>A two-way radio or satellite telephone in the vehicle has a link with the employer's central dispatch centre, which has voice communication via a telephone or radiotelephone with the hospital or BCEHS.</li>
        <li>The emergency vehicle is accompanied to the hospital by another vehicle that is equipped with a radio or satellite telephone or two-way radio that can contact the hospital directly and its driver can communicate with the emergency vehicle.</li>
        <li>In areas with good coverage, cell phones may be used.</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<p>In addition to the applicable first aid kit, an ETV should also contain the following: </p>
<table width="96%" border="0" cellpadding="2">
    <tbody>
        <tr>
            <th width="90%" valign="top" align="left">Description</th>
            <th width="10%" valign="top" align="left">Quantity</th>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Set of hard cervical collars covering all adult sizes (or 2 adjustable hard cervical collars), plus a head immobilizer</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Commercial lifting device with handholds, and securing straps to secure an injured worker</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Stretcher, basket stretcher, or other equipment appropriate for the worksite terrain to facilitate the transport of an injured worker &mdash; equipment should have retainer straps and a suitable mattress or padding</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">1</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Blankets &mdash; should be wool or similar fabric to ensure blankets can be used for padding and securing the patient as required</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">6</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Lower limb splints, minimum 1 m in length with suitable padding</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">2</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Vomitus bags</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">2</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 90%;">Cotton triangular bandages or other equipment to support limb and patient immobilization (such as quick straps)</td>
            <td valign="top" style="width: 10%;">6</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><strong>Industrial ambulance and mobile treatment centres</strong></p>
<p><strong>Industrial ambulance</strong><br />
In addition to the general recommendations for an ETV, an industrial ambulance should also have the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Be used only for first aid treatment and transportation of injured workers, under the direction of the first aid attendant</li>
    <li>Be capable of accommodating at least two workers on stretchers</li>
    <li>Have adequate lighting in the patient compartment, allowing the first aid attendant to see and assess the injured or ill worker and complete documentation without the use of a flashlight</li>
    <li>Contain a roll cot properly secured and cushioned against excessive jarring</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Mobile treatment centre (MTC)</strong><br />
An MTC is an industrial ambulance that also has the following:</p>
<ul style="list-style-type: disc;">
    <li>A sink with running water or, if this is not practicable, an alternative system for supplying fresh, potable water</li>
    <li>Minimum headroom of 1.8 metres (6 feet) in the treatment area, sufficient for the first aid attendant to treat the injured or ill worker</li>
    <li>Dressing station equipment</li>
</ul>
<p>As a result of an employer's first aid assessment, an MTC may be used in place of a first aid facility and emergency vehicle. When using an MTC in place of a dressing station or first aid room, the MTC should contain the same first aid equipment as outlined for those facilities. Where the workplace is more than 2 hours' surface travel time from a hospital, another vehicle suitable for transporting an injured worker (ETV or industrial ambulance) should also be provided.</p>
<p>It is not recommended to use an MTC as a first aid facility when the workplace provides overnight accommodation.</p>
<p><strong>BCEHS ambulance as emergency transport</strong><br />
<p>For rescue operations or other coordinated emergency response, a BCEHS ambulance is an acceptable alternative for an emergency transportation vehicle, if it is present at the workplace and available to transport injured workers.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:EmergencyVehicles" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:emergency-transportation" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16_1.1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16_1-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16(1)-2 Hospital and acute care facilities (formerly G3.16(1.2))</h6>
<p>Issued December 15, 2017; Editorial Revision November 1, 2024</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.16(1) of the <em>OHS Regulation</em> ("<em>Regulation</em>") states:
</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) An employer must provide for each workplace</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) at least the equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services required by Schedule 3-A, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) any additional equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services that are necessary to ensure that workers who suffer an injury at work can be</p>
<p class="subL3">(i) promptly provided first aid, and</p>
<p class="subL3">(ii) promptly transported to medical treatment.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to outline the requirements that will allow an acute care facility or hospital to use the emergency department area or, in the case of a diagnostic and treatment centre, the emergency resuscitation area, as a workplace first aid facility acceptable to WorkSafeBC.</p>
<p><strong>Requirements</strong><br />
An acute care health facility with an emergency department area or, in the case of a diagnostic and treatment centre, the emergency resuscitation area, may designate that area as the workplace first aid facility (dressing station or first aid room as required), provided that an assessment is conducted and recorded to ensure the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Prompt access to first aid services is available to all workers at all times (regardless of public wait times or triage issues)</li>
    <li>Confidentiality of first aid records is maintained</li>
    <li>The emergency area has at least one sink plumbed with hot and cold water within easy access of the patient care area</li>
    <li>Toilet facilities are located in or close to the emergency area for quick and easy access</li>
    <li>First aid procedures clarify how an injured worker who requires transport is to be moved from the location of injury to the designated treatment area. For example:
    <ul>
        <li>Contacting the BC Emergency Health Services (BC Ambulance Service) for transport within the facility, or</li>
        <li>Ensuring injured workers are transported using appropriate equipment, by workers who are trained in patient handling and transport.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16_1.2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16_2-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16(2)-2 Automated external defibrillator&nbsp;(AED)</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Retired November 1, 2024</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16_2-2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16_3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16(3) Reviewing first aid assessments</h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.16(3) of the <em>OHS Regulation</em> ("<em>Regulation</em>") states:
</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">The employer must review and update the assessment under subsection&nbsp;(2)</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) within 12 months after the previous assessment or review, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) whenever a significant change affecting the assessment occurs in the employer's operations.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to provide guidance on what changes would be considered "significant" for the purpose of triggering a review of a first aid assessment.</p>
<p><strong>Reviewing first aid assessments</strong><br />
Section 3.16(3) requires first aid assessments to be reviewed and updated every 12 months or whenever a significant change affecting the assessment occurs.</p>
<p>Significant changes in the employer's operations that would require an employer to review and update the first aid assessment include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Changes in the maximum number of workers present at the workplace</li>
    <li>The class of the workplace changes (accessibility or remoteness changes)</li>
    <li>The nature and extent of risks and hazards in the workplace changes through the introduction of new or different equipment, processes, tasks, or workplace conditions</li>
    <li>WorkSafeBC updates the hazard rating assigned to the employer's classification unit</li>
    <li>Where changes to barriers or routes are identified that may affect first aid response, transportation requirements, or BCEHS response or travel time</li>
</ul>
<p>Employers should record the date when a first aid assessment is reviewed and who is involved in the review process so compliance with the requirements of this section can be verified. If changes are required following the review, a new assessment document should be completed in consultation with workers.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16_3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.16_3.1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.16(3.1) Involving workers in first aid assessment</h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.16(3.1) of the <em>OHS Regulation</em> ("<em>Regulation</em>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">An assessment under subsection (2) must be prepared, reviewed and updated in consultation with the joint committee or the worker health and safety representative, as applicable.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to provide direction to employers on involving workers in preparation and review of first aid assessments.</p>
<p><strong>Involving workers in first aid assessments</strong><br />
Section 3.16(3.1) requires first aid assessments to be prepared, reviewed, and updated in consultation with the joint health and safety committee or worker health and safety representative.
<p>Workers who perform work are in the best position to identify hazards and risks associated with the tasks being performed, inform the employer of "near-miss" incidents that have occurred in the past, and identify potential challenges that may be experienced when accessing the workplace. As such, employers should encourage meaningful involvement of the joint committee or worker representative in conducting first aid assessments to establish the appropriate level of first aid equipment, training, and supplies.</p>
<p>Employers can enhance worker participation in conducting first aid assessment by: </p>
<ul>
    <li>Including the joint committee or worker representatives in workplace inspections and walk-through surveys to identify risks and hazards that may affect first aid assessments.</li>
    <li>Providing the joint committee or representative with information on past injuries and incidents, incident response, and drills to assist them in evaluating the effectiveness of current procedures and services.</li>
</ul>
<p>Where a workplace is required to have a joint committee (20 or more workers, or where required by order) or worker health and safety representative (more than 9 but fewer than 20 workers), these parties must be consulted in the development and review of first aid assessments. Where these parties are not familiar with the workplace or hazards being assessed, it may also be necessary to seek the input of other workers who have this knowledge.
