While completing the first aid assessment with the direct involvement of worker representatives is the preferred method of involvement, consultation with joint committees or worker representative may take various forms to accommodate operational requirements. Some options for consultation may include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Engage the joint committee or worker representative in developing task-specific assessments for common jobs performed by workers (as relevant). </li>
    <li>Develop a process to have the joint committee or worker representative regularly review first aid assessments conducted by crews and provide feedback on any concerns noted.</li>
    <li>Explore options for joint committee members or worker representatives to participate in assessments remotely by telephone or video call.</li>
    <li>Have joint committees designate alternative workers to assist the employer in conducting first aid assessments on the committee's behalf. (If this is done, it should be documented in the joint committee's terms of reference and a process for training and reporting back to the joint committee established).</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Small workplaces without a joint committee or designated worker health and safety representative </strong><br />
Smaller workplaces should also involve workers in the first aid assessment process. Worker input on assessments can be obtained during regular monthly meetings with workers (as required by <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.2">section 3.2</a> of the <em>Regulation</em>), as part of a job safety analysis, or during other meetings (e.g., tailgate meetings, toolbox talks, pre-job safety meetings).</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.16_3.1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.17" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.17 Developing and implementing first aid procedures</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018; Revised consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.17 of the <cite>Occupational Health and Safety Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) The employer must keep up-to-date written procedures for providing first aid at the workplace including</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) the equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services available,</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the location of, and how to call for, first aid,</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) how the first aid attendant is to respond to a call for first aid,</p>
<p class="subL2">(c.1) if there are any barriers to first aid being provided to
injured workers, how injured workers will be accessed and
moved,how the first aid attendant is to respond to a call for first aid,</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) the authority of the first aid attendant over the treatment of injured workers and the responsibility of the employer to report injuries to the Board,</p>
<p class="subL2">(e.1) if emergency transportation is required by section 3 of
Schedule 3-A, the location of the method of emergency
transportation, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(e) who is to call for transportation for the injured worker, and the methods of transportation and calling,</p>
<p class="subL2">(f) prearranged routes in and out of the workplace and to medical treatment.</p>
<p class="subL1">(2) The employer must post the procedures conspicuously in suitable locations throughout the workplace or, if posting is not practicable, the employer must adopt other measures to ensure that the information is effectively communicated to workers.</p>
<p class="subL1">(3) The first aid attendant and all other persons authorized to call for transportation for injured workers must be trained in the procedures.</p>
<p class="subL1">&hellip;</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to provide additional information on the development and implementation of written first aid procedures.</p>
<p><strong>First aid procedures</strong><br />
Written procedures must include the information required by section 3.17(1) of the <em>Regulation</em>:</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants, and services available</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Procedures should include the names of first aid attendants, where practicable, and their first aid certification level, as well as a description of first aid equipment and supplies available at the worksite.</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Coordination of multiple first aid attendants</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Some workplaces may require multiple first aid attendants. The purpose of multiple attendants is to ensure adequate first aid coverage and response to major incidents. When multiple designated attendants are present, the following guidance should be considered and incorporated in the written procedures:</p>
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>In workplaces where first aid attendants have different levels of certification, procedures should state that the designated attendant with the higher certification should be the lead attendant for serious incidents and serious injuries. For minor injuries, any designated attendant may provide care and complete the required documentation. Lower level attendants should consult with the higher level attendant to ensure that the higher level attendant has no additional treatment options to offer the injured worker prior to discharge.</li>
        <li>For work locations with multiple attendants with the same level of certification, procedures should include instructions on how to determine which attendant will be the lead attendant for major events, serious incidents, or serious injuries (for example, person who has been attendant for longest, first on scene, etc.).</li>
        <li>Where a workplace has multiple first aid attendants, procedures should identify how attendants are to respond (for example, attendants working and responding on different floors of a building). For major events, serious incidents, and serious injuries, the employer should consider a procedure to have multiple designated attendants respond to ensure optimal first aid treatment to injured workers.</li>
        <li>Employers must consider the requirements of <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.21">section 3.21(3)</a> when developing response procedures. Once care is initiated, an attendant cannot hand off care to an attendant with a lower level of certification.</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<ul>
    <li><strong>The location of, and how to call for, first aid</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Procedures should instruct workers on how to obtain first aid for both minor injuries and serious injuries. Procedures should also include instructions on the expected response of first aid attendants when it may be necessary to either transport the injured worker or request emergency services. The location of first aid equipment and supplies must be included in the procedures; a map is sometimes helpful for this purpose.</p>
<p>Methods of summoning first aid attendants may include whistles, sirens, lights, pagers, two-way radios, or portable or cell phones that a first aid attendant would see or hear and that would enable the first aid attendant to know where to respond.</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Instructions on how the first aid attendant is to respond to a call for first aid</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>In many workplaces, first aid attendants perform other tasks in addition to their duties as first aid attendants. If any procedures are required to enable the first aid attendant to leave their assigned duties, those should be included in the first aid procedures.</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Authority of the first aid attendant over the treatment of injured workers</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>First aid procedures should state that the first aid attendant is in charge of the first aid management of injured workers until responsibility of care is handed off to pre-hospital emergency medical personnel, medical aid (i.e., walk-in clinic or hospital), or a person with higher or equivalent first aid certification, or the first aid attendant has determined the injured worker may return to work (refer to OHS Guideline <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.21_3">G3.21(3)</a> for additional information). Workers have the right to refuse medical treatment (including first aid); however, an injured worker should be advised of the potential consequences of refusing care, and their refusal should be documented.</p>
<p>Supervisory personnel may not overrule a first aid attendant's decisions regarding first aid and the need for medical attention. This includes decisions on:
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>The necessity of transporting the worker to hospital</li>
        <li>The method of transportation</li>
        <li>The necessity of accompanying the injured worker during transportation</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Responsibility of the employer to report injuries to WorkSafeBC</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Section 150 of the <em>Workers Compensation Act</em> requires that employers report work-related injuries or illnesses to WorkSafeBC. To ensure  these requirements are met, first aid procedures should instruct workers to report all work related injuries or illnesses to their supervisor and/or to the first aid attendant &mdash; regardless of whether or not they wish to receive treatment (refer to OHS Guideline <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.19">G3.19</a> for additional information).</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Instructions on how injured workers will be accessed and moved to an area accessible to BCEHS</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Where the workplace (or areas of the workplace) are not accessible to BCEHS attendants, first aid procedures should detail equipment and personnel required to access and move an injured worker to an accessible location. For workplaces that have developed separate rescue procedures for less-accessible areas, first aid procedures may reference those. This element is not required if all of areas of the workplace are accessible to BCEHS.</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Procedures for transporting an injured worker to medical services</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>First aid attendants receive training on when to refer workers to medical aid. First aid procedures should include instruction for:</p>
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>How an injured worker will be transported to hospital or medical services (e.g., walk-in clinic) if the worker is ambulatory and their condition in stable and not life-threatening</li>
        <li>How an injured worker will be transported to hospital if the worker is not ambulatory, or their condition is unstable or life-threatening (criteria for rapid transport is included in Appendix G of WorkSafeBC's <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/resources/health-safety/books-guides/jurisprudence-package-obtaining-first-aid-certification-BC" target="_blank">jurisprudence package</a>)</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<p>Methods of transportation may include a taxi or ride-hail, or being driven by a co-worker in a company or other vehicle that is insured, maintained, and inspected for business use. Employers retain responsibility for ensuring injured workers are safely transported without undue delay, and must be able to show due diligence in this regard.</p>
<p>In addition to identifying the mode of transportation to be used, procedures should also include the following information:</p>
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>Who is responsible for arranging transportation for the injured worker</li>
        <li>Directions to the nearest hospital and non-emergency medical treatment facility</li>
        <li>Instructions for using equipment or procedures that may be used to safely move an injured worker to an area accessible by BCEHS (if applicable)</li>
        <li>Information BCEHS may require to access the workplace</li>
        <li>The location of emergency transportation, as applicable</li>
        <li>Who will communicate with the worker's emergency contact and what information may be shared</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Procedures for transporting a worker from remote workplaces</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>In remote workplaces &mdash; where BCEHS ambulance services may be significantly delayed, or where the nearest medical facility available to treat workers is more than two hours away from the workplace (considering hospital bypass protocols that may be in place) &mdash; employers should consider providing alternative or supplementary methods of emergency transport for seriously injured workers, where crew size makes this practicable. This may mean employers provide and maintain a vehicle meeting the requirements of Schedule 3-A section 6, or arrange for the availability of emergency air transportation (refer to OHS Guideline <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.20">OHS Guideline G3.17</a> for additional information on air transport). This procedure is only required for remote work locations.</p>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Maintaining first aid services</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Procedures should also include roles and responsibilities for ensuring appropriate first aid services are maintained and available to the workplace. This may include assigning responsibilities for:</p>
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>Scheduling and assigning first aid attendants to ensure adequate coverage for each shift</li>
        <li>Managing work or obtaining alternative first aid services in the event of an unplanned absence of a first aid attendant</li>
        <li>Tracking and scheduling recertification of first aid attendants, and replacing them when they are absent or leave the employer or assigned work location</li>
        <li>Regularly inspecting first aid supplies and equipment and replenishing supplies as they are used</li>
        <li>Inspecting and maintaining emergency transportation vehicles</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<ul>
    <li><strong>Ensuring workplace first aid programs are inclusive of all workers</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Workers seeking first aid treatment have the right to fair and equitable treatment by first aid attendants. Employers are responsible for ensuring that all workers are treated with respect and without discrimination, including both those seeking first aid, and those providing first aid services.</p>
<p>Training first aid attendants and other workers in the principles and practices of equity, diversity, and inclusion (EDI), and integrating these principles into an employer's first aid program, can enhance the effectiveness and inclusivity of the program. The following are some examples how EDI principles may be considered when developing or enhancing a first aid program:</p>
<ul>
    <ul>
        <li>Equipment &mdash; Ensure that the equipment available, especially personal protective equipment (PPE), is suitable for workers of various sizes. For example, first aid kits often come with large-sized gloves, which may not fit all first attendants.</li>
        <li>Accessibility &mdash; Ensure that signage and written procedures are provided in languages accessible to the workforce. Develop processes to bridge language gaps or other barriers between first aid attendants and the workforce they support.</li>
        <li>Worker engagement &mdash; Support processes to routinely gather feedback from all workers, and make enhancements to the first aid program accordingly.</li>
    </ul>
</ul>
<p>More information on EDI principles and practices can be found in the <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://bchumanrights.ca/resources/employment-equity-toolkit/" target="_blank">Employment equity toolkit</a> from the BC's Office of the Human Rights Commissioner.</p>
<p><strong>Training workers on first aid procedures</strong><br />
Training workers on first aid procedures is required as part of new worker orientation (refer to <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.23">section 3.23(h)</a> of the <em>Regulation</em>) and procedures should be regularly reviewed with workers to ensure they are understood by all.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.17" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.17_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.17(2) Communicating first aid procedures to workers </h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.17(2) of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(2) The employer must post the procedures conspicuously in suitable locations throughout the workplace or, if posting is not practicable, the employer must adopt other measures to ensure that the information is effectively communicated to workers.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to provide examples of other measures to ensure information is effectively communicated to workers.</p>
<p><strong>Other means of communicating first aid procedures</strong><br />
It is important for all workers to understand the employer's first aid procedures and be able to easily access these procedures for reference in the event of an incident involving injury to a worker. Where practicable, first aid procedures should be physically printed and posted throughout the workplace. However, on temporary worksites or where workers work away from the head office, posting procedures may not be the most effective means of ensuring workers read, understand, and have quick access to first aid procedures.</p>
<p>Other methods of making procedures readily accessible to workers may include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>For workers who use regularly use cell phones to communicate with the workplace, provide procedures by QR code, website, app, or other mobile-friendly platform.</li>
    <li>For office-based workers, or workers who have ready access to a computer, procedures may be made available on an intranet or shared drive. Be sure workers are trained on how to find first aid procedures and that they can be found quickly. Confirm this as part of drill and/or workplace inspections.</li>
    <li>For mobile workers, consider providing copies of first aid procedures in vehicles. Include a confirmation of procedure documents on vehicle checklists or inspections.</li>
    <li>Include copies of first aid procedures in first aid kits or with other first aid supplies or equipment.</li>
</ul>
<p>Involve workers in the selection of communication methods that will work best for the workforce. A combination of methods may be needed to ensure first aid procedures have been effectively communicated to all workers.</p>
<p>Construction projects and multiple-employer workplaces may have first aid procedures available in the first aid room.</p>
<p><strong>Translation of first aid procedures</strong><br />
Employers are responsible for ensuring workers have access to and understand workplace health and safety information. Employers with workers who are not fluent in English should consider having first aid procedures and other relevant information translated into workers' primary language(s) where necessary.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.17_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.17_4" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.17(4) Drills </h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.17(4) of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(4) At least once each year and whenever the procedures change, the employer must ensure that drills are conducted to ensure that</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the procedures are effective, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) workers, first aid attendants and other persons referred to in subsection (3) are capable of fulfilling their roles and responsibilities.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to provide guidance around the requirement for first aid drills, and what factors should be considered when designing drills.</p>
<p><strong>Drills</strong><br />
Drills for first aid provide the employer and workers with an opportunity to test the effectiveness of first aid procedures. Drills provide workplace parties with a chance to practice their roles and responsibilities, which helps to ensure an effective response in the event of a real emergency.</p>
<p>Drills also provide an opportunity to identify and resolve potential challenges or deficiencies in the employer's written first aid procedures.</p>
<p>Procedures and processes that should be assessed during first aid drills include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The ability of first aid attendants or other workers to access equipment, supplies, and first aid facilities</li>
    <li>Workers' familiarity with how to call for first aid and the location of first aid equipment</li>
    <li>How effectively the first aid attendant(s) can be summoned and respond to both minor and serious injuries</li>
    <li>How effectively an injured worker can be accessed and moved, and if there are any barriers present in the workplace</li>
    <li>How effectively an injured worker can be prepared (packaged) and transported to an area accessible to BC Emergency Health Services (BCEHS), if required</li>
    <li>Whether first aid attendants are familiar with documentation and reporting requirements</li>
</ul>
<p>A first aid drill should include mock scenarios that support the evaluation of the effectiveness of the procedures listed above. If the workplace requires first aid equipment and procedures that exceed the minimum requirements of Schedule 3-A, those procedures should be incorporated into the annual drill.</p>
<p>BCEHS should not be contacted for the purpose of conducting first aid drills. Information on what to expect when calling 911 that can be incorporated into drills is available on the BCEHS website.</p>
<p><strong>Worker safety during drills</strong><br />
Drills should reasonably approximate the expected response to an incident requiring first aid, up to the point of transport to hospital or medical aid. Drills should be designed in such a way that they do not pose an undue hazard to workers, first aid attendants participating in the drill, or the public. Where necessary to ensure worker safety, dummies may be used to simulate injured workers when testing procedures for transporting an injured worker to an ETV or area accessible to BCEHS. If a drill of a particular first aid procedure poses an undue hazard to workers or others, a tabletop exercise may be used to supplement drill activities, if it will allow for an effective evaluation of the employers' procedures.</p>
<p><strong>Post-drill</strong><br />
The results of the drill and any subsequent changes to first aid procedures should be shared with all first aid attendants, as well as with the joint health and safety committee or worker health and safety representative as applicable.</p>
<p><strong>When to conduct drills</strong><br />
Drills should be conducted as soon as practicable following the implementation of procedures, and thereafter at least annually.</p>
<p>Drills must also be conducted when there are substantive changes to written first aid procedures. Examples of procedural changes that will generally meet the criteria of "substantive" include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Change in required transportation methods (e.g., taxi to ETV)</li>
    <li>Substantial change in the method for summoning the designated first aid attendant (e.g., switching from two-way radio to a flashing light)</li>
    <li>Change in the workplace class (e.g., class 2 - remote to class 3 - less-accessible)</li>
    <li>Change to the level of first aid certification required (e.g., basic to advanced)</li>
    <li>Change to rescue procedures that affect first aid procedures</li>
</ul>
<p>Changes that would not generally be considered substantial include:</p>
<ul>
    <li>First aid procedures that are the same, but with varied locations, for medical aid (i.e., walk-in clinic or hospital)</li>
    <li>Changes to the location of the closest ambulance station without impacting the workplace class</li>
    <li>Variations in who is acting as designated first aid attendant</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Workplace and workforce considerations </strong><br />
The following table provides guidance on when drills should be conducted based on the characteristics of the workplace or workforce.</p>
<table class="tableData" border="1" cellspacing="2" cellpadding="2">
    <tbody>
        <tr bgcolor="#cccccc">
            <th>Workplace characteristics</th>
            <th>Drill considerations</th>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Single or multiple employer<sup>*</sup> fixed location</td>
            <td>
            <ul>
                <li>Annually at each fixed location, and when procedures change</li>
            </ul>
            </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>Single or multiple employer<sup>*</sup> working at various temporary locations<sup>**</sup></td>
            <td>
            <ul>
                <li>Annual drill at one location, provided procedures remain substantively the same</li>
                <li>If procedures vary substantially between locations, then multiple drills will be required</li>
            </ul>
            </td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>
<br />
<p><sup>*</sup>For multiple-employer locations, drills should be initiated by the prime contractor unless there are other arrangements made in writing<br />
<sup>**</sup> For the purpose of conducting drills, a temporary location will be considered any location where workers are located for less than a year.</p>
<p>Note: Reviewing a real incident requiring first aid will not generally be considered an acceptable alternative to a drill as this practice may discourage the identification of deficiencies and/or introduce privacy or other concerns for injured workers, first aid attendants, and employers.</p>
<p><strong>Documenting first aid drills</strong><br />
Employers should maintain drill records to establish compliance with the requirements of section 3.17(4) of the <em>Regulation</em>. Drill records should contain the following information:
<ul>
    <li>Date and time of drill</li>
    <li>Names and roles of participants</li>
    <li>Brief summary of scenario tested</li>
    <li>Summary of observations and outcomes, including any deficiencies identified</li>
    <li>Any corrective actions taken</li>
</ul>
<p>If significant deficiencies are observed, or the drill cannot be conducted as intended, employers should address any deficiencies and perform the drill again, if procedures change significantly.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.17_4" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.17.1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.17.1 Air transportation</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Revised consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.17.1 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>If air transportation is the primary or only method for transporting an injured worker, all of the following requirements must be met:</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) before the start of operations in a workplace, arrangements must be made with an air service to ensure that</p>
<p class="subL3">(i) an appropriate aircraft is reasonably available to the workplace during those operations, and;</p>
<p class="subL3">(ii) without limiting subparagraph (i), there is at least</p>
<p class="subL4">(A) one stretcher in the aircraft, or</p>
<p class="subL4">(B) one stretcher, at the workplace, that is compatible with the aircraft;</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the arrangements in paragraph (a) must include procedures for</p>
<p class="subL3">(i) the employer to determine the availability of appropriate aircraft before the start of each work day, and</p>
<p class="subL3">(ii) the air service to notify the employer if an appropriate aircraft ceases to be available;</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) a system must be provided that enables the pilot of the aircraft and the first aid attendant attending to an injured worker to communicate at all times when the aircraft is in transit to the location of the injured worker and during transport of the injured worker to medical treatment.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to outline the arrangements required for air transport of injured or ill workers.</p>
<p><strong>Air transport</strong><br />
If air transport is the only reasonably means of accessing the work location, employers must arrange for emergency air transport for seriously injured workers. Employers should also plan to use air transport as the primary method of transporting a seriously injured worker to hospital if air transport is the primary means of accessing the work location or when the surface travel time to hospital is greater than two hours.</p>
<p>If air transport is the method used to transport an injured worker to medical treatment, the following arrangements and equipment are recommended:</p>
<ul>
    <li>A list of radio frequencies to be used between the air carrier and the workplace should be included in the written procedures required by section 3.17(1) of the <em>Regulation</em>.</li>
    <li>The coordinates of the workplace &mdash; or another method to determine and communicate the coordinates of the workplace to the air carrier &mdash; should be included in the written procedures.</li>
    <li>First aid equipment should be suitable for the aircraft to be used, including a stretcher or lifting device that will fit in the aircraft and that does not allow movement or excessive jarring of the injured or ill worker during air transport. Employers are responsible for ensuring that first aid attendants are properly trained in the use of the equipment.</li>
    <li>Ensure an appropriate landing spot for the aircraft exists that will not endanger the pilot or people on the ground, unless provisions are in place for long-line extraction.</li>
    <li>Ensure all persons who may be involved in the loading of an injured person into an aircraft (not just first aid attendants) are adequately trained in the procedures associated with that activity.</li>
</ul>
<p>Refer to the WorkSafeBC bulletin <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/resources/health-safety/hazard-alerts/air-evacuation-in-forestry-operations" target="_blank">Air evacuation in forestry operations</a> for additional considerations.</p>
<p>Where air transportation may not be readily available at all times (such as during poor weather conditions, where there is lack of suitable landing site, or when air transport may be temporarily unavailable), employers should develop alternative land transport plans wherever practicable. In determining which method of transport should be used to transport seriously injured workers to medical care when both are available, air transportation should be used if it is likely to save 30 minutes or more in total transport time. The decision on which method of transport should be used rests with the first aid attendant, taking into account risks and benefits for the injured or ill worker.</p>
<p>If air transportation is the only method of transport, employers should avoid conducting high-risk work when air transportation is unavailable.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.17.1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.18_1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.18(1) Communications</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018</p>
<p>Section 3.18(1) of the <cite>Occupational Health and Safety Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) The employer must provide an effective means for</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) communication between the first aid attendant and the workers served, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the first aid attendant to call for assistance.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to outline suitable means of communication between the first aid attendant and workers.</p>
<p><strong>Effective communication</strong><br />
There is an "effective means" of communication if workers throughout the workplace know how to alert the first aid attendant that service is required. That system could consist of a whistle, siren, series of lights, pager, two-way radios, or portable phones that the first aid attendant would see or hear and that would enable the first aid attendant to know where to respond.</p>
<p><strong>Assistance</strong><br />
"Assistance" in section 3.18(1)(b) may include assistance from other workers, the BC Ambulance Service, or another ambulance service acceptable to WorkSafeBC.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.18_1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.18_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.18(2) Availability of first aid attendant</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018; Editorial Revision consequential to November&nbsp;1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p>Section 3.18(2) of the <cite>Occupational Health and Safety Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The employer must not assign, and the first aid attendant must not undertake, employment activities that will interfere with the attendant's ability to receive and respond to a request for first aid.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline discusses how to ensure that the first aid attendant is available to render first aid promptly, as required by sections&nbsp;3.16(1) and 3.18(2) of the <em>Regulation</em>.</p>
<p><strong>Availability</strong><br />
In order to provide effective treatment, the equipment, facilities, and attendant must be accessible and first aid must be administered to the worker as soon as practicable after the injury or illness, in accordance with the practices and standards found in the first aid attendant's training program.</p>
<p>The following principles apply in determining whether the first aid service has been properly provided:</p>
<ul>
    <li>A first aid attendant should actually be present in the area served, during all working hours. This includes periods such as lunch or coffee breaks when workers are on shift and at the workplace but not actually working.</li>
    <li>When multiple attendants are required by Schedule 3-A, it is expected that the required number of first aid attendants will normally be present in the workplace during all working hours covered by the first aid assessment. First aid attendants can provide cross-coverage for scheduled breaks.</li>
    <li>First aid attendants, equipment, and facilities must be ready to receive the injured worker or to depart to where the worker is situated without delay, usually within 3 to 5 minutes of being summoned. (This allows the first aid attendant time to clean up as needed, either take off coveralls or put on clean coveralls, and obtain the first aid kit.) </li>
    <li>The location of the central first aid service should be readily accessible. A service will be readily accessible where it is within 10 minutes' walking time (or driving time, where vehicles are normally used for general movement within the workplace) for all workers in a workplace. Alternatively, the service is readily accessible where the first aid attendant can reach injured workers within 10 minutes' walking time (or driving time) to render first aid. </li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Backup for absent first aid attendant</strong><br />
Absences from the workplace by first aid attendants may be planned (such as vacations or medical appointments) or unplanned (such as travelling with an injured worker to hospital or being absent because of sickness). Since it is foreseeable that planned and unplanned absences will occur, the employer will be expected to have a procedure for dealing with them.</p>
<p>If a first aid attendant is unexpectedly absent, an employer is expected to have a replacement first aid attendant in place within half a shift. If no first aid attendant is available due to unplanned absence, employers should avoid conducting high-risk work at that time. For workplaces that require first aid attendants with different levels of certification, high-risk work should be avoided when the first aid attendant with the higher level of certification is absent.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.18_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.19" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.19 First aid records</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Revised March 5, 2013; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018; Editorial Revision November 1, 2024</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.19 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) The employer must maintain at the workplace, in a form acceptable to the Board, a record of all injuries and exposures to contaminants covered by this Regulation that are reported or treated.</p>
<p class="subL1">(2) First aid records must be kept for at least 3 years.</p>
<p class="subL1">(3) First aid records are to be kept confidential and may not be disclosed except as permitted by this Regulation or otherwise permitted by law.</p>
<p class="subL1">(4) First aid records must be available for inspection by an officer of the Board.</p>
<p class="subL1">(5) Workers may request or authorize access to their first aid records for any treatment or report about themselves.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline outlines what form of record-keeping is acceptable to WorkSafeBC and what access to records is needed.</p>
<p><strong>Acceptable record-keeping</strong><br />
Records containing the following information are acceptable to WorkSafeBC:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The full name and occupation of the worker</li>
    <li>The date and time of injury or report of exposure or illness</li>
    <li>The date and time the injury, exposure, disease, or illness was reported to the employer or employer's representative</li>
    <li>The names of witnesses</li>
    <li>A description of how the injury, exposure, disease, or illness occurred</li>
    <li>A description of the nature of the injury, exposure, disease, or illness</li>
    <li>A description of the treatment given and any arrangements made relating to the worker (e.g., return to work, medical aid, ambulance, follow-up)</li>
    <li>A description of any subsequent treatment given for the same injury, exposure, disease, or illness</li>
    <li>The signature or equivalent of the attendant or person giving first aid, and if possible, the signature of the worker receiving treatment</li>
</ul>
<p>A <a rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.worksafebc.com/en/resources/health-safety/forms/first-aid-record-external-form-55b23" target="_blank">first aid record form</a> is available on worksafebc.com. Employers do not have to use this form as long as the records they use capture the required information.</p>
<p><strong>Revising first aid records</strong><br />
First aid records must be completed as thoroughly as possible by a first aid attendant as soon as practicable after completing treatment. In small workplaces that do not require a first aid attendant, or when workers are injured away from the workplace (if the worker does not receive treatment from a first aid attendant), a first aid record may be completed by another person who administers care, or a supervisor. First aid records should not be revised or updated by anyone other than the person who created the record, except to add administrative information that was unavailable at the time of the incident. Any revisions made to first aid records should be made in a clear and traceable way (e.g., single cross-out with change initialed).</p>
<p><strong>Access to records</strong><br />
Only people who have a legitimate business need to review first aid records may have access. For example this may include the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>A worker's direct supervisor</li>
    <li>The injured worker, or a designated representative (access to their own records only)</li>
    <li>A person designated by the employer to manage or audit health and safety, compensation claims, and/or return-to-work programs at the workplace</li>
    <li>First aid attendants at the workplace</li>
    <li>Prime contractors, if required for the purpose of coordinating health and safety of the workplace</li>
    <li>A WorkSafeBC prevention officer</li>
</ul>
<p>Where a person is entitled to access first aid records, access may not extend to all records. First aid records should be treated as personal medical information, and anyone accessing these records must keep the information confidential in accordance with applicable privacy legislation. Access is limited to the minimum information necessary to satisfy the purpose for which access is required &mdash; for example, if access is required to investigate a claim for compensation, it would be limited to the records of the individual making the claim.</p>
<p>Joint committee members and worker health and safety representatives generally do not need full access to first aid records. A report containing a summary of the records is generally sufficient for joint committee purposes. Prime contractors should also be provided with summary information, where sufficient for the required purpose.</p>
<p>First aid records are owned by the employer of the injured worker. In a multiple-employer worksite, records may be maintained by the employer providing the first aid services, however records must be provided to the injured worker's employer once the employer's work on the project is complete. First aid records must be retained by the injured worker's employer for at least three years.</p>
<p>If records are kept and stored electronically, they should be stored within Canada.
</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.19" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.20" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.20 Multiple employer workplaces</h6>
<p>Issued March 30, 2004; Editorial Revision February 1, 2008; Editorial Revision November 1, 2024</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.20 of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>If workers of 2 or more employers are working at a workplace at the same time, the prime contractor must</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) conduct an assessment of the circumstances of the workplace under section 3.16(2) in relation to all the workers in the workplace, and</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) do everything that is reasonably practicable to establish and maintain the first aid equipment, supplies, facilities, first aid attendants and services required under section 3.16.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
The purpose of this guideline is to discuss the role of the prime contractor in providing first aid services. It also considers situations where a group of employers with adjacent workplaces provide a common first aid service.</p>
<p><strong>Role of the prime contractor</strong><br />
On multiple-employer workplaces, the prime contractor will normally set up a central first aid service for the whole workplace or arrange for a subcontractor to do this. The prime contractor is the person defined under the <em>Workers Compensation Act</em> ("<em>Act</em>") as the owner of the workplace unless the owner enters into a written agreement with another party to assume the responsibilities of the prime contractor.</p>
<p>Where first aid service is provided by agreement with another person or persons, the following guidance applies:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The first aid services provided must be appropriate for the hazard rating, workplace class, and number of workers for each of the employers who may utilize the service (refer to <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.16">OHS Guideline G3.16</a>)</li>
    <li>The service provided should be adequate, taking into account the anticipated needs of all employers using the service. The calculation for determining the service level is based on the total number of workers across all workplaces per shift. Additionally, it is important to factor in any usage of the service by members of the public who may be visiting these workplaces (refer to OHS Guideline G3.16 &ndash; Barriers to first aid).</li>
    <li>Employers must be informed of any changes or temporary deficiencies in first aid services that may impact an employer's compliance with first aid requirements.</li>
    <li>When deciding on the appropriate location for first aid services, prioritize the swift provision of first aid. Additionally, assess whether certain workplaces pose higher risks, leading to a potentially increased demand for the service.</li>
    <li>The level and location of the service must consider any barriers to access that may occur during peak work periods.</li>
    <li>Each employer participating in the service must separately comply with the obligation in <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.19">section 3.19(1)</a> of the <em>Regulation</em> to maintain records of all injuries and exposures to contaminants at their own workplace. The service may also keep central records.</li>
    <li>The service and each employer must restrict access to any first aid records to the persons authorized by section 3.19. Any person who has access to the records under section 3.19 must keep them confidential except as required for the legitimate purpose of their access.</li>
</ul>
<p>Despite any arrangement made by a prime contractor, the employer retains full legal responsibility for ensuring appropriate first aid services are available to the employers' workers as required under <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.16">section 3.16</a> of the <em>Regulation</em>. If the level of first aid service does not meet this requirement, WorkSafeBC will hold the employer responsible, not the person agreeing to supply the service.
</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.20" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.21_1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.21(1) Suspension and cancellation of first aid certificates</h6>
<p>Issued August 31, 2007; Editorial Revision September 6, 2018; Editorial Revision April 6, 2020; Editorial Revision November 1, 2024</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.21(1) of the <em>OHS Regulation ("Regulation")</em> states, in part:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) The first aid attendant must</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) promptly provide injured workers with a level of care within the scope of the attendant's training and this Part,</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) objectively record observed or reported signs and symptoms of injuries and exposures to contaminants covered by this Regulation</p>
<p class="subL2">&hellip;</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Section 96 of the <em>Workers Compensation Act ("Act")</em> states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(1) If the Board has reasonable grounds for believing that a person who holds a certificate issued under the OHS provisions or the regulations has breached a term or condition of the certificate or has otherwise contravened an OHS provision or a provision of the regulations, the Board may, by order,</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) cancel or suspend the certificate, or</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) place a condition on the use of that certificate that the Board considers necessary in the circumstances.</p>
<p class="subL1">(2) An order under this section suspending a certificate must specify the length of time that the suspension is in effect or the condition that must be met before the suspension is no longer in effect.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline sets out the circumstances in which WorkSafeBC will consider suspending or cancelling a first aid certificate, and discusses the process  by which first aid certificates are suspended or cancelled.</p>
<p><strong>Background</strong><br />
Occupational first aid certificates are issued to first aid attendants by first aid agencies on behalf of WorkSafeBC. These agencies enter into an agreement with WorkSafeBC that permits them to issue first aid certificates to individuals.</p>
<p>WorkSafeBC has the authority to suspend or cancel these first aid certificates under section&nbsp;96 of the <cite>Act</cite>.</p>
<p>Where a WorkSafeBC certification or prevention officer learns of circumstances that may indicate a lack of competence or misconduct on the part of a first aid attendant, the officer may consider suspending the first aid attendant's certificate. The Manager of Certification Services may then consider further action, which could involve cancellation of the certificate.</p>
<p><strong>When may a first aid certificate be suspended or cancelled?</strong><br />
Under section&nbsp;96 of the <cite>Act</cite>, WorkSafeBC may cancel or suspend a first aid certificate where it has <em>"reasonable grounds for believing"</em> that the holder has</p>
<ul>
    <li>Breached a term or condition of the certificate</li>
    <li>Contravened an OHS provision of the <cite>Act</cite> or the <cite>Regulation</cite></li>
</ul>
<p>Section 3.21 of the <cite>Regulation</cite> sets out the requirements for the first aid attendant. The failure of a first aid attendant to meet these obligations would be a contravention of the <cite>Regulation</cite> for the purposes of section&nbsp;96 and may provide grounds for the suspension or cancellation of the certificate. Such circumstances would include failing to</p>
<ul>
    <li>Promptly provide injured workers with a level of care within the scope of the first aid attendant's training and in accordance with sections <a href="/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#87772020C04D4BD3818D605A1918E211">3.14 through 3.21</a> of the <cite>Regulation</cite></li>
    <li>Objectively record observed or reported signs and symptoms of injuries and exposures to contaminants</li>
    <li>Refer for medical treatment workers with injuries considered by the first aid attendant as being serious or beyond the scope of the first aid attendant's training</li>
    <li>Be physically and mentally capable of safely and effectively performing the required duties</li>
</ul>
<p>Policy item <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-policies/policies-for-the-workers-compensation-act#SectionNumber:P2-96-1">P2-96-1</a> sets out an additional list of inappropriate conduct for first aid attendants, which may be considered to be failing to provide workers with an appropriate level of care under section&nbsp;3.21, including the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li> Smoking while assessing or treating an injured worker and/or while handling oxygen therapy  equipment, or permitting others to do so</li>
    <li> Failing to use the assessment and injury treatment techniques outlined in first aid training courses unless conditions precluded them</li>
    <li> Conduct that poses an unreasonable threat to the safety and well-being of other workers or the public</li>
    <li> Removing themselves from being able to see or hear any summons for first aid at a workplace</li>
    <li> Abandoning an injured worker after beginning assessment or treatment</li>
    <li> Refusing to treat an injured worker when acting as a designated first aid attendant</li>
    <li> Treating or transporting an injured worker while impaired or under the influence of drugs or alcohol</li>
</ul>
<p>Failing to provide competent care, failing to ensure first aid records are kept, and using intoxicants while on duty are common grounds for suspending and/or cancelling of certificates.</p>
<p><strong>Process for suspending or cancelling a first aid certificate</strong><br />
There are two stages to suspending and/or cancelling of an occupational first aid certificate. The first stage, a temporary suspension, involves a WorkSafeBC officer seizing the certificate from the first aid attendant and forwarding it to the Manager of Certification Services. The second stage involves the Manager of Certification Services reviewing the circumstances leading to the suspension and making a determination on what further action should be taken. The Manager of Certification Services may cancel the certificate, return it to the first aid attendant, or place conditions based on the review.</p>
<p><em>Stage 1: Officer's interim suspension</em><br />
Before a first aid certificate may be suspended or cancelled, WorkSafeBC must have "reasonable grounds for believing" that a contravention of the <cite>Act</cite> or <cite>Regulation</cite> or a breach of the terms of the first aid attendant's certificate has occurred. While a finding of "reasonable grounds" does not require absolute proof that circumstances amounting to non-compliance have occurred, it does require that the WorkSafeBC officer undertake an investigation of the circumstances in question to ensure the suspension or cancellation is reasonable.</p>
<p>The officer's investigation should include the following:</p>
<ol>
    <li>Review the employer's incident investigation document</li>
    <li>Review the worksite written procedures to ensure there is clear direction for attendant response</li>
    <li>Inspect attendant training and orientation records</li>
    <li>Provide the attendant an opportunity to offer his/her account of the circumstances</li>
    <li>Interview all persons who may have relevant information before making the decision to suspend the certificate</li>
</ol>
<p>Once the investigation is complete, and the officer thinks there are reasonable grounds for concluding that the first aid attendant has failed to comply with the <cite>Act</cite>, <cite>Regulation,</cite> or the terms of the certificate, the officer will conduct the following:</p>
<ol>
    <li>Issue an Order to Worker suspending the first aid certificate<br />
    <br />
    This order must specify the length of time that the suspension is in effect (as required by section&nbsp;96(2) of the <cite>Act</cite>). The duration of the suspension may be up to seven days pending review by the Manager of Certification Services.<br />
    <br />
    Note: The officer may also consider issuing orders to the employer or other workplace parties in connection with the circumstances, as appropriate.</li>
    <br />
    <li> Notify the employer of the suspension of the certificate.</li>
    <br />
    <li> Forward evidence supporting the suspension, any evidence offered by the attendant in his/her defense, and the outcome of the employer's investigation to Certification Servicess</li>
</ol>
<p><em>Stage 2: Cancellation or other action</em><br />
Once the officer's evidence is forwarded to Certification Services, the manager of Certification Services will undertake a review of the circumstances and make a final determination with respect to the first aid certificate. This may include issuing a warning, placing a condition on the certificate, further suspension, or cancellation of the certificate.</p>
<p>The type of action the Manager of Certification Services takes will depend on the circumstances of each case. Factors that will be considered include the following:</p>
<ul>
    <li>The risk of harm to workers caused by the breach, the potential severity of that harm, and the number of workers put at risk</li>
    <li>The potential for future risk to workers should the attendant be allowed to continue to provide services</li>
    <li>Whether the breach was caused by carelessness, recklessness, willful blindness, or intentional</li>
    <li>The need to maintain public or stakeholder confidence in first aid services in general</li>
</ul>
<p>Once the Manager of Certification Services has made a determination, a letter is forwarded to the first aid attendant notifying him or her of the Manager's decision and specifying the conditions under which reinstatement may occur. The letter also advises of the right to appeal. A "Request for Review" form is included with the letter.</p>
<p>The employer is notified if the first aid attendant's certificate is cancelled. The training agency that issued the certificate is also notified of the cancellation.</p>
<p><strong>Suspending or revoking acceptance of alternative licences</strong><br />
<em>Stage 1: Officer's interim suspension</em><br />
If a WorkSafeBC officer has reasonable grounds for concluding that a medical licence holder acting as a first aid attendant has failed to comply with the <em>Act</em>, <em>Regulation</em>, or the terms of recognition outlined in the guideline, the officer will conduct the following actions:</p>
<ol>
    <li>Issue an Order to Worker to the first aid attendant under section 22(1) of the <em>Act</em><br />
    The order must specify that the attendants may not act as a first aid attendant for seven days pending review by the manager of Certification Services.</li>
    <li>Issue an inspection report to the employer notifying them of the suspension and issuing other related orders to the employer or other workplace parties in connection with the circumstances, as appropriate.</li>
    <li>Forward evidence supporting the suspension, any evidence offered by the attendant in their defense, and the outcome of the employer's investigation to Certification Services</li>
</ol>
<p><em>Stage 2: Cancellation or other action</em><br />
Once the officer's evidence is forwarded to Certification Services, the manager of Certification Services will review of the circumstances and, if warranted, refer the matter to the appropriate licensing board for investigation and appropriate corrective actions. If this action is taken, the Order to Worker should be extended until such time as the licensing board completes their investigation of the matter. WorkSafeBC may revoke the acceptance of an individual medical licence holder as a first aid attendant, however only the applicable licensing board may impose conditions on the terms of the individual's medical licence.</p>
<p><strong>Review and appeal</strong><br />
<a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/workers-compensation-act/part-6-review-of-board-decisions#SectionNumber:Part6Sec268">Section 268(1)(a)</a> of the <cite>Act</cite> provides that a person may request a review officer to review "a Board order respecting an occupational health or safety matter under the OHS provisions, a refusal to make such an order, or a variation or cancellation of such an order."</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.21_1" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.21_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.21(2) Attendant fit for duty</h6>
<p>Issued September 6, 2018; Editorial Revision November 1, 2024</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.21(2) of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(2) A first aid attendant must be physically and mentally capable of safely and effectively performing the required duties, and the Board may at any time require the first aid attendant to provide a medical certificate.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline is to outline the fitness requirements for candidates and for occupational first aid attendants.</p>
<p><strong>Attendant fit for duty</strong><br />
A first aid attendant must be physically and mentally capable of safely and effectively performing the required duties. If a first aid attendant is temporarily incapable of performing their duties to injury, illness, or other factors, the employer must reassign the role.</p>
<p><strong>Medical certificate</strong><br />
To determine that a first aid attendant is physically and mentally capable of safely and effectively performing the required duties, a certification of medical fitness may be required by WorkSafeBC for attendants of any certification level. If required, a medical certificate of the first aid attendant's or candidate's fitness must be submitted from a physician on a form acceptable to WorkSafeBC. Payment for the medical examination is the responsibility of the first aid attendant or candidate, unless an alternative agreement exists between a worker or workers and an employer to the effect that the employer will be responsible for payment either at no cost or some cost to the worker.</p>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.21_2" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<a name="SectionNumber:G3.21_3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.21(3) First aid attendant responsibilities &ndash; Options for discharging responsibilities for care</h6>
<p>Issued consequential to November 1, 2024 Regulatory Amendment</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.21(3) of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p class="subL1">(3) The first aid attendant is responsible, and has full authority, for the provision of first aid to an injured worker until responsibility for treatment is accepted</p>
<p class="subL3">(a) at a place of medical treatment,</p>
<p class="subL3">(b) by ambulance personnel, or</p>
<p class="subL3">(c) by a person with higher or equivalent first aid certification.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline provides clarity around the options first aid attendants have for discharging their responsibility under this section, particularly at workplaces where there may be multiple first aid attendants with differing levels of first aid certification.
<p><strong>Options for discharging responsibility for care</strong><br />
If a worker is seriously injured (meets criteria for rapid transport provided as part of first aid training) or non-ambulatory, the first aid attendant with the highest level of training should provide care during transport, unless responsibility for care is transferred to a person with an equivalent or higher level of certification, a BC Emergency Health Services (BCEHS) paramedic, or a place of medical treatment.</p>
<p>For less serious injuries, once first aid has been rendered and the injured worker is determined to be stable, ambulatory, and not in need of additional first aid, the first aid attendant has the following options for discharging their responsibility for treatment:</p>
<ul>
    <li>Refer worker to medical treatment &ndash; If the first aid attendant, in consultation with the injured worker, determines that care during transport is not required, then a worker may be transported to medical treatment by a supervisor or other worker (including a first aid attendant with less training).(Note: Workers have the right to refuse medical treatment &ndash; refer to OHS Guideline <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-guidelines/guidelines-part-03#SectionNumber:G3.17">G3.17.</a>)</li>
    <li>Advise the worker they may return to work &ndash; If the first aid attendant determines that the worker's injury does not require additional medical attention beyond first aid, the attendant may discharge the worker back to work. Workers always have the option to seek medical treatment beyond the level of first aid for all injuries.</li>
</ul>
<a name="EndSectionNumber:G3.21_3" style="padding: 0px;"></a>




                <h4>Guidelines - Part 3 - Young or New Workers</h4>
<ul class = "navHorizPipe navRegTools"><li><a href="/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#561D251586F84701A997C19D033ABDBE"/>Related Regulations</a></li><li><a class='add_remove add_to_handbook' name='426F6D0913DB46DFBBD73EB7C1C5C9CA' id='426F6D0913DB46DFBBD73EB7C1C5C9CA' href='#'><span class='handbook_title'>+ Add to My Handbook</a></span></li></ul><a name="SectionNumber:G3.23" style="padding: 0px;"></a>
<h6>G3.23 Young or new worker orientation and training </h6>
<p>Issued July 26, 2007; Revised July 9, 2009; Editorial Revision consequential to August 4, 2015 Regulatory Amendment; Editorial Revision April 6, 2020</p>
<p><strong>Regulatory excerpt</strong><br />
Section 3.23 (Young or new worker orientation and training) of the <cite>OHS Regulation</cite> ("<cite>Regulation</cite>") states:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>3.23  Young or new worker orientation and training</strong></p>
<p class="subL1">(1) An employer must ensure that before a young or new worker begins work in a workplace, the young or new worker is given health and safety orientation and training specific to that young or new worker's workplace.</p>
<p class="subL1">(2) The following topics must be included in the young or new worker's orientation and training:</p>
<p class="subL2">(a) the name and contact information for the young or new worker's supervisor;</p>
<p class="subL2">(b) the employer's and young or new worker's rights and responsibilities under the <cite>Workers Compensation Act</cite> and this <cite>Regulation</cite> including the reporting of unsafe conditions and the right to refuse to perform unsafe work;</p>
<p class="subL2">(c) workplace health and safety rules;</p>
<p class="subL2">(d) hazards to which the young or new worker may be exposed, including risks from robbery, assault or confrontation;</p>
<p class="subL2">(e) working alone or in isolation;</p>
<p class="subL2">(f) violence in the workplace;</p>
<p class="subL2">(g) personal protective equipment;</p>
<p class="subL2">(h) location of first aid facilities and means of summoning first aid and reporting illnesses and injuries;</p>
<p class="subL2">(i) emergency procedures;</p>
<p class="subL2">(j) instruction and demonstration of the young or new worker's work task or work process;</p>
<p class="subL2">(k) the employer's health and safety program, if required under section 3.1 of this <cite>Regulation</cite>;</p>
<p class="subL2">(l) WHMIS information requirements set out in Part 5, as applicable to the young or new worker's workplace;</p>
<p class="subL2">(m) contact information for the occupational health and safetycommittee or the worker health and safety representative, as applicable to the workplace.	</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Purpose of guideline</strong><br />
This guideline is to assist employers and workers in implementing the requirements of section 3.23. It provides information on
</p>
<ul>
    <li> The objectives of the orientation and training requirements listed in section 3.23 </li>
    <li> Possible means of delivery of these requirements</li>
    <li> Where to get further information to assist with the orientation</li>
</ul>
<p>The guideline also provides an overview of age-related requirements regarding children in the workplace under the BC <cite>Employment Standards Act</cite>, for information only, should this issue arise at a workplace.</p>
<p><strong>Objectives of the orientation topics</strong><br />
Under section 3.23 employers will be required to provide young and new workers with orientation and training about safe work procedures and how to recognize hazards on the job. It lists a number of topics that must be addressed.</p>
<p>In many workplaces some of the requirements in section 3.23 will already be in place as part of the general safety measures in the workplace. To conduct proper orientation, the topics must be provided to young and new workers.</p>
<p>  There may be topics beyond those listed in section 3.23 that an employer would wish to include in the orientation. The <cite>Regulation</cite> sets a minimum standard, which employers may exceed. In some cases, one or more of the topics may not be applicable in a given workplace and would not need to be included.</p>
<p>In the discussion below any reference to "worker" means "young or new worker." </p>
<p><strong>(a) Name and contact information for the worker's supervisor</strong><br />
The worker must know the identity of the individual(s) responsible for providing work direction to him/her, and how to contact him/her if they are not immediately available. This can be particularly helpful to ensure any ongoing questions in the early period of time on the job are addressed. </p>
<p><strong>(b) The employer's and worker's rights and responsibilities</strong><br />
The worker must be informed about his/her rights and responsibilities and those of the employer under the <cite>Workers Compensation Act</cite> ("<em>Act</em>") and the <cite>Regulation</cite>. For example, the worker has the right to be informed about workplace hazards (including WHMIS), the duty to report hazards, the duty to refuse unsafe work, and the right to participate in workplace health and safety activities. The worker should also be advised of the protection from prohibited action provisions in the <cite>Act</cite>, and provisions related to first aid and reporting any injuries and diseases.  </p>
<p><strong>(c) Workplace health and safety rules</strong><br />
The worker must be trained in the workplace health and safety rules applicable to the workplace and the tasks the worker will perform. The rules are expected to address any hazards that the worker may encounter, including various types of controls, such as work procedures, use of personal protective equipment, and the safe means of operating equipment.  </p>
<p><strong>(d) Hazards to which the worker may be exposed</strong><br />
The worker must be informed about the hazards he/she could encounter while performing assigned work tasks. Depending on the work setting, these hazards may be physical in nature and involve a risk of injury, or may pose a risk of disease (e.g., when handling a hazardous substance). If a worker is in a location that involves contact with the public, the employer must advise of any risks that may arise, including, as applicable, abusive behaviour, robbery, assault, or other possible confrontation.  </p>
<p><strong>(e) Working alone or in isolation</strong><br />
If the worker is assigned to work alone or in isolation, the worker must be trained in the policies and procedures to be followed. Under the requirements of the <cite>Regulation</cite> the employer must set up a system for checking on the well being of the worker. When establishing the system, the employer must consult with the worker on the time intervals to be used. In some cases working alone is linked to a potential for violence in the workplace. </p>
<p><strong>(f) Violence in the workplace</strong><br />
The worker must be provided with orientation and training on the policies and procedures to be followed in the event of violence in the workplace. The worker should be advised of the meaning of the term "violence," which includes any threatening statement or behaviour, and the circumstances in the workplace where a risk of violence may be present. The worker should be trained in the procedures to follow to eliminate or minimize any risk in such situations, for example, when handling money, and opening or closing the business. He/she should also be trained in the steps to take to eliminate or minimize the risk of injury to the worker in the event of an incident.  </p>
<p>
In part, this topic is already covered under topics (c), (d), and (e). However, instruction in this topic will ensure that the worker is given an understanding of the overall measures in the workplace for protection from violence.</p>
<p><strong>(g) Personal protective equipment (PPE)</strong><br />
The worker must be provided with appropriate orientation and training in the use and care of any personal protective equipment or clothing that the worker  is required to use to safely perform his/her work. This is also a requirement under <a href="/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-08-personal-protective-clothing-and-equipment#93A561D36AE44A1787F7AF30573D6AF4">Part 8</a> of the <cite>Regulation</cite>, and will help the worker meet his or her obligations to use PPE properly. </p>
<p><strong>(h) Location of first aid facilities, the means of summoning first aid, and reporting illnesses and injuries</strong><br />
The worker must be advised of the location of first aid facilities, the identity of the first aid attendant(s), and how to summon an attendant. This topic also covers the employer's obligation to inform the worker of the procedures to follow to report an illness or injury to WorkSafeBC. </p>
<p><strong>(i) Emergency procedures</strong><br />
The worker must be advised of potential emergency situations that could occur in his/her work location, and trained in the procedures to follow. This topic is a companion to topic (h) on first aid, and addresses other aspects of emergency response, such as evacuation in the event of fire, or if hazardous substances are handled, how to contain a spill of the substance. </p>
<p><strong>(j) Instruction and demonstration of the worker's work task or work process</strong><br />
The worker must be provided with both instruction and demonstration - not simply a verbal description - of work tasks that the worker will be required to perform when he/she begins work. Further training may be required as new tasks are assigned. </p>
<p>
The demonstration should address the aspects of the work that will involve safety risks if not performed correctly. For example, if the worker will be operating a piece of mechanical equipment, the employer will need to ensure that all safety points are demonstrated, including the use of guarding and other safety devices, means of equipment startup, and how to follow safe operating procedures.</p>
<p><strong>(k) The employer's occupational health and safety (OHS) program</strong><br />
Under this topic the employer is expected to provide an orientation to the OHS program in the workplace. If a program is required under section <a href="/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#1CEAD44ADD5D4A2D9B5C845A0A278637">3.1</a> of the <cite>Regulation</cite> the orientation would describe the program elements, which are outlined in section <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.3">3.3</a> of the <cite>Regulation</cite>, and how they are implemented. If, for a small workplace, the program is less formal, then the orientation would be on the elements of the program outlined in section <a href="/en/law-policy/occupational-health-safety/searchable-ohs-regulation/ohs-regulation/part-03-rights-and-responsibilities#SectionNumber:3.2">3.2</a>. </p>
<p><strong>(l) WHMIS information requirements, as applicable to the worker's workplace</strong><br />
This topic is intended to ensure the worker is provided with an orientation on the Workplace Hazardous Materials Information System (WHMIS), and its application to hazardous products in the workplace. The orientation should explain the WHMIS hazard classes, and the use of WHMIS labels and Safety Data Sheets (SDS). In addition, there are four WHMIS objectives for training a worker in how to work safely with hazardous products. Workers need to know the hazards of the products, how they can protect themselves, what to do in case of an emergency or spill, and where to get more information on the products. The first three of these will already be addressed under other topics such as (c), (d), (h), and (i). To address the fourth, typically workers will need to be informed of where SDSs are located or how they can be accessed if available electronically. </p>
<p>
If there are hazardous products in the workplace not covered by WHMIS, the orientation under topics such as (c), (d), (h), and (i) should be given to address safety with those products.</p>
<p><strong>(m) Contact information for the joint occupational health and safety (OHS) committee, or worker health and safety representative</strong><br />
If applicable, the employer must inform the worker on how to contact the joint OHS committee, or the worker health and safety representative. </p>
<p><strong>Delivering the orientation</strong><br />
The employer must determine how to deliver the orientation and training to the worker. However, there are a number of options to consider.
</p>
<ul>
    <li> <strong><em>Address topics according to applicability:</em></strong> As previously noted, some topics listed in section 3.23 may not be applicable in a given workplace. The employer can adjust the orientation accordingly. </li>
    <li> <strong><em>Organize topics into groups:</em></strong> Section 3.23 requires that applicable topics be covered in the orientation or training, but not necessarily as separate items. The employer can organize the orientation or training in any manner, as long as the content intended by the topics is addressed. For example, three of the topics involve contact information, and could be presented as a unit. Two of the topics (first aid and emergency procedures) involve a common theme of emergency response. The topics on working alone and violence often cover aspects of the same issue, and could be presented together. Other combinations are also possible. </li>
    <li> <strong><em>Use generic instruction and orientation coupled with site-specific information:</em></strong> Information on some of the topics listed in section 3.23 may be applicable from one workplace to another while site-specific instructions will only apply at the worksite in question.  Generic instruction and orientations can serve as a good basis on which an employer can add employer or site-specific information. Generic instruction and orientation, coupled with site-specific information can be particularly  useful where a worker is performing the same work under different circumstances. Examples include circumstances where
    <ul>
        <li> Employers have a number of workplaces</li>
        <li> The industry has highly mobile workers, such as in construction</li>
        <li> Workers are performing casual or temporary work, such as substitute teachers</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<p>In determining the right combination of generic and site-specific topics that will meet the requirements of section 3.23, the circumstances of each scenario need to be considered. By way of example, generic topics for workers under the above noted circumstances could include
</p>
<ul>
    <li> Employer and worker rights and responsibilities</li>
    <li> Employer's occupational health and safety program</li>
    <li> Generic aspects of WHMIS </li>
    <li> Personal protection equipment</li>
</ul>
<p>Topics that will be specific to a site include
</p>
<ul>
    <li> Workplace health and safety rules</li>
    <li> Name and contact of supervisor</li>
